| BY ANTHONY MUNDAY AND | HENRY CHETTLE, WITH REVISIONS AND ADDITIONS BY THOMAS DEKKER, WILLIAM SHAKESPEARE AND THOMAS HEY WOOD |
| THE | text that follows is entirely different from any other in this volume. All the other plays derive from printed editions; this comes from what is probably the untidiest, most heavily revised dramatic manuscript of the period, giving us unique insights into its playwrights’ working conditions. It represents a troubled and ultimately abandoned attempt on the part of various authors to create a script, interrupted by the censorial intervention of the Master of the Revels, [[Edmund]] Tilney. The working manuscript preserved in the British Library is described on its first leaf as ‘The Booke’—that is, the theatre manuscript—‘of Sir Thomas Moore.’ The basic manuscript is a fair copy made by the dramatist Anthony Munday (1560-1633) of a text in which he may have collaborated with Henry Chettle (c. 1560-c. 1607). Alterations and additions were made by Chettle, Thomas Dekker (c. 15 72-1632), very probably William Shakespeare, and probably Thomas Heywood (c. 1573-1641). A theatre scribe annotated parts of the manuscript, and some of the revisions exist in transcripts he wrote out. In this edition each section is preceded and concluded with an identification of the hand.<br>It seems likely that the original play was written during the early 1590s and submitted in the usual way to the Master of the Revels for a licence. Tilney called for substantial alterations. Though the play’s favourable portrait of a man sometimes seen as a Catholic martyr was provocative, Tilney’s attention was concentrated mostly on the insurrection scenes. In our view the original play was laid aside until soon after [[Queen Elizabeth]] died, in 1603, when the political objections would have carried less weight, and the revisions—which do not meet Tilney’s requirements—were made then. Shakespeare’s authorship of the majority of Sc.6, first proposed in 1871, has been accepted by most scholars on the basis of handwriting and of the evidence of dramatic and linguistic style. His contribution shows him as a thoroughgoing professional sharing with colleagues whose work he respected in an essentially collaborative enterprise.<br>[[Sir Thomas More]] is based on Holinshed’s Chronicles and Nicholas Harpsfield’s [[Biography\|biography]] of More. Sheriff More peacefully quells the riots of Londoners against resident foreigners on the ‘Ill May Day’ of 1517, and is appointed Lord Chancellor as a reward. In the Shakespearian Sc. 6, More persuades the rebels to surrender to the [[King]], arguing for obedience to authority and challenging the rebels to consider their own plight if, like the strangers, they were to live in [[Exile\|exile]]. A passage less securely attributed to Shakespeare is More’s bemused and wary soliloquy at the beginning of Sc. 8. The play elsewhere presents a series of serio-comic episodes dramatizing his wit in attempting to [[Reform\|reform]] minor offenders, his credentials as a humanist, his practical joking, and his [[Love\|love]] of plays. More’s downfall and passage to the scaffold begin when he refuses the [[King]]’s demand that he sign unspecified articles. Later scenes, though sombre in tone, depict his almost light-hearted resolution to pursue [[Death\|death]] rather than yield to the [[King]]’s demands.<br>The play has been performed most notably by the Royal Shakespeare Company in 2005. |

| DOLL | Williamson<br>WILLIAMSON, her husband, a carpenter |
| CLOWN | BETTS, his brother, called Ralph<br>SHERWIN, a goldsmith<br><br>Francis de BARDE, a Lombard<br>CAVELIER, a Lombard or Frenchman<br><br>The [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] of [[London]]<br>The LADY MAYORESS<br>Justice SURESBY<br>LIFTER, a cutpurse<br>SMART, the plaintiff against him<br>The RECORDER of [[London]]<br><br>Sir Thomas PALMER<br>Sir Roger CHOLMLEY<br>Sir John MUNDAY<br>A SERGEANT-at-arms<br>CROFTS, a messenger from the [[King]]<br><br>RANDALL, More’s manservant<br>Jack FALKNER, a ruffian<br>ERASMUS, a learned clerk of Rotterdam<br>MORRIS, secretary to the Bishop of Winchester<br>The Lord Cardinal’s PLAYERS, performing the roles of: |

| NOTE ON SPECIAL FEATURES | OF PRESENTATION<br>Complexities in the manuscript have led to the following modifications of standard [[Oxford]] Shakespeare presentation.<br><br>Rules across the column show where the text switches from the Original Text to a revision and back, or from one revision to another. Notes in the right margin above and below these rules specify the section of the manuscript and the hand.<br><br>Annotations in a second hand are preserved and printed in a special typeface. Passages in the manuscript explicitly or implicitly deleted are underlined in this edition, except where the final text as edited duplicates the deleted text (see Appendix B). Identifiable interventions by second hands are as follows:<br><br>Tilney: in Sc. 1, Sc. 3, Sc. 10.<br>Playhouse book-keeper: in Sc. 4, Sc. 6 (Addition II and 1. 234), Sc. 9.<br>Dekker: phrase at 8.232-3. Heywood(?): addition of Clown’s part in Sc. 6 and Sc. 7.<br><br>Angle brackets 〈 〉 indicate gaps in the text due to damage to the manuscript. |
| *(didascalia)* | Stage directions reflect the wording of the manuscript unless enclosed in special brackets  . |
| NOTE ON SPECIAL FEATURES | The Book of [[Sir Thomas More]]<br>[Original Text (monday)]<br>[Tilney]<br>Leave out the insurrection wholly and the cause thereof, and begin with Sir Thomos More at the Mayor’s sessions, with a report afterwards of his good service done being Sheriff of [[London]] upon a mutiny against the Lombards—only by a short report, and not otherwise, at your own perils. E. Tilney.<br><br>Sc. 1 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter at one end John [[Lincoln]] with George Betts and Clown Betts together. At the other end enters Francis de Barde and Doll, a lusty woman, he hauling her by the arm |
| DOLL | Whither wilt thou haul me? |
| BARDE | Whither I please. Thou art my prize, and I plead purchase of thee. |
| DOLL | Purchase of me? Away, ye rascal! I am an honest, plain carpenter’s wife, and, though I have no [[Beauty\|beauty]] to like a husband, yet whatsoever is mine scorns to stoop to a stranger. Hand off then when I bid theel |
| BARDE | Go with me quietly, or I’ll compel thee. |
| DOLL | Compel me, ye dog’s face? Thou think‘st thou hast the goldsmith’s wife in hand, whom thou enticed’st from her husband with all his plate, and when thou turned‘st her [[Home\|home]] to him again mad’st him, like an ass, pay for his wife’s board. |
| BARDE | So will I make thy husband too, if please me. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Cavelier, with a pair of doves, Williamson the carpenter and Sherwin following him |
| DOLL | Here he comes himself. Tell him so if thou dar’st. |
| CAVELIER | [to Williamson] Follow me no further. I say thou shalt not have them. |
| WILLIAMSON | I bought them in Cheapside, and paid my money for them. |
| SHERWIN | He did, sir, indeed, and you offer him wrong, both to take them from him and not restore him his money neither. |
| CAVELIER | If he paid for them, let it suffice that I possess them. Beefs and brewis may serve such hinds. Are pigeons meat for a coarse carpenter? |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | [ aside to George Betts] It is hard when Englishmen’s patience must be thus jetted on by strangers, and they not dare to [[Revenge\|revenge]] their own wrongs. |
| GEORGE BETTS | [aside to [[Lincoln]]] [[Lincoln]], let’s beat them down, and bear no more of these abuses. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | [aside to George Betts] We may not, Betts. Be patient and hear more. |
| DOLL | How now, husband? What, one stranger take thy food from thee, and another thy wife? By’r Lady, flesh and [[Blood\|blood]], I think, can hardly brook that. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | Will this gear never be otherwise? Must these wrongs be thus endured? |
| GEORGE BETTS | Let us step in, and help to [[Revenge\|revenge]] their injury. |
| BARDE | What art thou that talkest of [[Revenge\|revenge]]? My Lord Ambassador shall once more make your Mayor have a check if he punish thee not for this saucy presumption. |
| WILLIAMSON | Indeed my [[Lord Mayor]] on the Ambassador’s complaint sent me to Newgate one day because, against my will, I took the wall of a stranger. You may do anything. The goldsmith’s wife, and mine now, must be at your commandment. |
| GEORGE BETTS | The more patient fools are ye both to suffer it. |
| BARDE | Suffer it? Mend it thou or he if ye can or dare. I tell thee, fellow, an she were the Mayor of [[London]]’s wife, had I her once in my possession I would keep her in spite of him that durst say nay. |
| GEORGE BETTS | I tell thee, Lombard, these words should cost thy best cap, were I not curbed by duty and obedience. The Mayor of [[London]]’s wife? O God, shall it be thus? |
| DOLL | Why, Betts, am not I as dear to my husband as my [[Lord Mayor]]’s wife to him, [ to Williamson] and wilt thou so neglectly suffer thine own shame? [To de Barde ] Hands off, proud stranger, or, by Him that bought me, if men’s milky hearts dare not strike a stranger, yet women will beat them down ere they bear these abuses. BARDE Mistress, I say you shall along with me. |
| DOLL | Touch not Doll Williamson, lest she lay thee along on God’s dear earth. (To Cavelier) And you, sir, that allow such coarse cates to carpenters, whilst pigeons which they pay for must serve your dainty appetite: deliver them back to my husband again, or I’ll call so many women to mine assistance as we’ll not leave one inch untorn of thee. If our husbands must be bridled by law, and forced to bear your wrongs, their wives will be a little lawless, and soundly beat ye. |
| CAVELIER | Come away, de Barde, and let us go complain to my Lord Ambassador. Exeunt both |
| DOLL | Ay, go, and send him among us, and we’ll give him his welcome too. I am ashamed that free-born Englishmen, having beaten strangers within their own bounds, should thus be braved and abused by them at [[Home\|home]]. |
| SHERWIN | It is not our lack of [[Courage\|courage]] in the cause, but the strict obedience that we are bound to. I am the goldsmith whose wrongs you talked of; but how to redress yours or mine own is a matter beyond all our abilities. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | Not so, not so, my good friends. I, though a mean man, a broker by profession, and named John [[Lincoln]], have long [[Time\|time]] winked at these vile enormities with mighty impatience, and, as these two brethren here, Bettses by name, can witness, with loss of mine own life would gladly remedy them. |
| GEORGE BETTS | And he is in a good forwardness, I tell ye, if all hit right. |
| DOLL | As how, I prithee? Tell it to Doll Williamson. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | You know the Spital sermons begin the next week. I have drawn a bill of our wrongs, and the strangers’ insolencies. |
| GEORGE BETTS | Which he means the preachers shall there openly publish in the pulpit. |
| WILLIAMSON | O, but that they would! I’[[Faith\|faith]], it would tickle our strangers thoroughly. |
| DOLL | Ay, and if you men durst not undertake it, before God, we women will. Take an honest woman from her husband? Why, it is intolerable. |
| *(didascalia)* | SHERWIN to [[Lincoln]] But how find ye the preachers affected to our proceeding? |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | Master Doctor Standish means not to meddle with any such matter in his sermon, but Doctor Beal will do in this matter as much as a priest may do to [[Reform\|reform]] it, and doubts not but happy [[success]] will ensue upon our wrongs. You shall perceive there’s no hurt in the bill. Here’s a copy of it. I pray ye, hear it. |
| ALL THE REST | With all our hearts. For God’s sake, read it. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | (reads) ‘To you all the worshipful lords and masters of this [[City\|city]], that will take compassion over the poor people your neighbours, and also of the great importable hurts, losses, and hindrances whereof proceedeth extreme [[Poverty\|poverty]] to all the [[King]]’s subjects that inhabit within this [[City\|city]] and suburbs of the same. For so it is that aliens and strangers eat the bread from the fatherless children, and take the living from all the artificers, and the intercourse from all merchants, whereby [[Poverty\|poverty]] is so much increased that every man bewaileth the misery of other; for craftsmen be brought to beggary, and merchants to neediness. Wherefore, the premises considered, the redress must be of the commons, knit and united to one part. And as the hurt and damage grieveth all men, so must all men set to their willing power for remedy, and not suffer the said aliens in their wealth, and the natural-born men of this region to come to confusion.’ |
| DOLL | Before God, ’tis excellent, and I’ll maintain the suit to be honest. |
| SHERWIN | [[Well]], say ’tis read, what is your further meaning in the matter? |
| GEORGE BETTS | What? Marry, list to me. No [[Doubt\|doubt]] but this will store us with friends enough, whose names we will closely keep in writing, and on May Day next in the morning we’ll go forth a-Maying, but make it the worst May Day for the strangers that ever they saw. How say ye? Do ye subscribe, or are ye faint-hearted revolters? |
| DOLL | Hold thee, George Betts, there’s my hand and my heart. By the Lord, I’ll make a captain among ye, and do somewhat to be talked of for ever after. |
| WILLIAMSON | My masters, ere we part let’s friendly go and drink together, and swear true secrecy upon our lives. |
| GEORGE BETS | There spake an angel. Come, let us along then. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |
| GEORGE BETS | Sc. 2 |
| *(didascalia)* | An arras is drawn, and behind it, as in sessions, sit the [[Lord Mayor]], Justice Suresby, and other Justices, and the Recorder, Sheriff More and the other Sheriff sitting by. Smart is the plaintiff, Lifter the prisoner at the bar |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | Having dispatched our weightier businesses,<br>We may give ear to petty felonies.<br>Master Sheriff More, what is this fellow? |
| MORE | My lord, he stands indicted for a purse.<br>He hath been tried; the jury is together. |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | Who sent him in? |
| SURESBY | That did I, my lord.<br>Had he had right, he had been hanged ere this,<br>The only captain of the cutpurse crew. |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | What is his name? |
| SURESBY | As his profession is: Lifter, my lord,<br>One that can lift a purse right cunningly. |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | And is that he accuses him? |
| SURESBY | The same, my lord, whom, by your [[Honour\|honour]]’s leave,<br>I must say somewhat too, because I find<br>In some respects he is [[Well\|well]] worthy blame. |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | Good Master Justice Suresby, speak your mind.<br>We are [[Well\|well]] pleased to give you audience. |
| SURESBY | Hear me, Smart. Thou art a foolish fellow.<br>If Lifter be convicted by the law,<br>As I see not how the jury can acquit him,<br>I’ll stand to’t thou art guilty of his [[Death\|death]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | MORE to the [[Lord Mayor]] |
| SURESBY | My lord, that’s worth the hearing. |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | Listen then, good Master More. |
| *(didascalia)* | SURESBY to Smart |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | I tell thee plain, it is a shame for thee<br>With such a sum to tempt necessity.<br>No less than ten pounds, sir, will serve your turn<br>To carry in your purse about with ye,<br>To crack and brag in taverns of your money?<br>I promise ye, a man that goes abroad<br>With an intent of [[Truth\|truth]], meeting such a booty,<br>May be provoked to that he never meant.<br>What makes so many pilferers and felons<br>But such fond baits that foolish people lay<br>To tempt the needy miserable wretch?<br>Ten pounds odd money, this is a pretty sum<br>To bear about, which were more safe at [[Home\|home]].<br>[[Lord Mayor]] and More whisper<br>‘Fore God, ’twere [[Well\|well]] to fine ye as much more,<br>To the relief of the poor prisoners,<br>To teach ye be more careful of your own.<br>( ) rightly served.<br>( ) |
| MORE | Good my lord, sooth a ( ) for once,<br>Only to try conclusions in this case. |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | Content, good Master More. We’ll rise a while,<br>And till the jury can return their verdict<br>Walk in the [[Garden\|garden]]. How say ye, justices? |
| ALL JUSTICES | We like it [[Well\|well]], my lord; we’ll follow ye. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Lord Mayor]] and Justices |
| MORE | Nay, plaintiff, go you too. Exit Smart<br>And officers,<br>Stand you aside, and leave the prisoner<br>To me a while. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but More and Lifter |
| MORE | Lifter, come hither. |
| LIFTER | What is your worship’s pleasure? |
| MORE | Sirrah, you know that you are known to me,<br>And I have often saved ye from this place<br>Since first I came in office. Thou seest beside<br>That Justice Suresby is thy heavy friend,<br>For all the blame that he pretends to Smart<br><br>For tempting thee with such a sum of money.<br><br>I tell thee what: devise me but a means<br>To pick or cut his purse, and on my credit,<br>And as I am a Christian and a man,<br>I will procure thy pardon for that jest. |
| LIFTER | Good Master Sheriff, seek not my overthrow.<br>You know, sir, I have many heavy friends,<br>And more indictments like to come upon me.<br>You are too deep for me to deal withal.<br>You are known to be one of the wisest men<br>That is in England. I pray ye, Master Sheriff,<br>Go not about to undermine my life. |
| MORE | Lifter, I am true subject to my [[King\|king]].<br>Thou much mistak’st me, and for thou shalt not think<br>I mean by this to hurt thy life at all,<br>I will maintain the act when thou hast done it.<br>Thou knowest there are such matters in my hands<br>As, if I pleased to give them to the jury,<br>I should not need this way to circumvent thee.<br>All that I aim at is a merry jest.<br>Perform it, Lifter, and expect my best. |
| LIFTER | I thank your worship, God preserve your life!<br>But Master Justice Suresby is gone in.<br>I know not how to come near where he is. |
| MORE | Let me [[Alone\|alone]] for that. I’ll be thy setter.<br>I’ll send him hither to thee presently,<br>Under the colour of thine own request<br>Of private matters to acquaint him with. |
| LIFTER | If ye do, sir, then let me [[Alone\|alone]].<br>Forty to one but then his purse is gone. |
| MORE | [[Well]] said; but see that thou diminish not<br>One penny of the money, but give it me.<br>It is the cunning act that credits thee. |
| LIFTER | I will, good Master Sheriff, I assure thee. Exit More<br>I see the purpose of this gentleman<br><br>Is but to check the folly of the Justice<br><br>For blaming others in a desperate case,<br><br>Wherein himself may fall as soon as any.<br><br>To save my life it is a good adventure.<br>[[Silence]] there, hol Now doth the Justice enter. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Justice Suresby |
| SURESBY | Now, sirrah, now, what is your will with me?<br>Wilt thou discharge thy [[Conscience\|conscience]], like an honest<br>man?<br>What sayst to me, sirrah? Be brief, be brief. |
| LIFTER | As brief, sir, as I can.<br>(Aside) If ye stand fair, I will be brief anon. |
| SURESBY | Speak out and mumble not. What sayst thou, sirrah? |
| LIFTER | Sir, I am charged, as God shall be my comfort,<br>With more than’s true— |
| SURESBY | Sir, sir, ye are indeed, with more than’s true,<br>For you are flatly charged with felony.<br>You’re charged with more than [[Truth\|truth]], and that is theft:<br>More than a true man should be charged withal.<br>Thou art a varlet; that’s no more than true.<br>Trifle not with me, do not, do not, sirrah.<br>Confess but what thou knowest; I ask no more. |
| LIFTER | There be, sir—there be, if’t shall please your worship— |
| SURESBY | ‘There be’, varlet? What be there, tell me what there be?<br>Come off or on ‘there be’, what be there, knave? |
| LIFTER | There be, sir, divers very cunning fellows<br>That while you stand and look them in the face<br>Will have your purse. |
| SURESBY | Thou’rt an honest knave.<br>Tell me, what are they, where they may be caught.<br>Ay, those are they I look for. |
| LIFTER | You talk of me, sir—<br>Alas, I am a puny. There’s one, indeed,<br>Goes by my name; he puts down all for purses |
| SURESBY | Be as familiar as thou wilt, my knave.<br>’Tis this I long to know. |
| LIFTER | (aside)<br>And you shall have your [[Longing\|longing]] ere ye go.—<br>This fellow, sir, perhaps will meet ye thus, |
| *(didascalia)* | Action of greeting him |
| LIFTER | Or thus, or thus, and in kind compliment<br>Pretend acquaintance, somewhat doubtfully,<br>And these embraces serve. |
| SURESBY | (shrugging gladly)<br>Ay, marry, Lifter, wherefore serve they? |
| LIFTER | Only to feel<br>Whether you go full under sail or no,<br>Or that your lading be aboard your barque. |
| SURESBY | In plainer English, Lifter, if my purse<br>Be stored or no? |
| LIFTER | Ye have it, sir. |
| SURESBY | Excellent, excellent! |
| LIFTER | Then, sir, you cannot but for manners’ sake<br>Walk on with him, for he will walk your way,<br>Alleging either you have much forgot him,<br>Or he mistakes you. |
| SURESBY | But in this [[Time\|time]] has he my purse or no? |
| *(didascalia)* | Not yet, sir, fie! Aside No, nor I have not yours.— |
| *(didascalia)* | He takes Suresby’s purse. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Lord Mayor]], Justices, and the Recorder; Sheriff |
| *(didascalia)* | More and the other Sheriff |
| LIFTER | But now we must forbear; my lords return. |
| SURESBY | A murrain on’t! Lifter, we’ll more anon.<br>Ay, thou sayst true: there are shrewd knaves indeed.<br>He sits down<br>But let them gull me, widgeon me, rook me, fop me,<br>I‘[[Faith\|faith]], i’[[Faith\|faith]], they are too short for me.<br>Knaves and fools meet when purses go.<br>Wise men look to their purses [[Well\|well]] enough. |
| MORE | (aside)<br>Lifter, is it done? |
| LIFTER | (aside)<br>Done, Master Sheriff, and there it is. |
| *(didascalia)* | He gives Suresby’s purse to More |
| MORE | (aside)<br>Then build upon my word, I’ll save thy life. |
| RECORDER | Lifter, stand to the bar. 150<br>The jury have returned thee guilty; thou must die.<br>According to the custom, look to it, Master Sheriff. |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | Then, gentlemen, as you are wont to do,<br>Because as yet we have no burial place,<br>What [[Charity\|charity]] your meaning’s to bestow 155<br>Toward burial of the prisoners now condemned,<br>Let it be given. There is first for me. |
| RECORDER | And there’s for me. |
| ANOTHER | And me. |
| SURESBY | [[Body]] of me,<br>My purse is gone! |
| MORE | Gone, sir? What, here? How can that be? |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | Against all reason: sitting on the bench? 160 |
| SURESBY | Lifter, I talked with you; you have not lifted me, ha? |
| LIFTER | Suspect ye me, sir? O, what a world is this! |
| MORE | But hear ye, Master Suresby. Are ye sure<br>Ye had a purse about ye? |
| SURESBY | Sure, Master Sheriff, as sure as you are there; 165<br>And in it seven pounds odd money, on my [[Faith\|faith]]. |
| MORE | Seven pounds odd money? What, were you so mad,<br>Being a wise man, and a magistrate,<br>To trust your purse with such a liberal sum?<br>Seven pounds odd money? Fore God, it is a shame 170<br>With such a sum to tempt necessity.<br>I promise ye, a man that goes abroad<br>With an intent of [[Truth\|truth]], meeting such a booty,<br>May be provoked to that he never thought.<br>What makes so many pilferers and felons 175<br>But these fond baits that foolish people lay<br>To tempt the needy, miserable wretch?<br>Should he be taken now that has your purse,<br>I’d stand to‘t, you are guilty of his [[Death\|death]];<br>For, questionless, he would be cast by law.<br>’Twere a good deed to fine ye as much more,<br>To the relief of the poor prisoners,<br>To teach ye lock your money up at [[Home\|home]]. |
| SURESBY | [[Well]], Master More, you are a merry man.<br>I find ye, sir, I find ye [[Well\|well]] enough. |
| MORE | Nay, ye shall see, sir, trusting thus your money,<br>And Lifter here in trial for like case,<br>But that the poor man is a prisoner,<br>It would be now suspected that he had it.<br>Thus may ye see what mischief often comes<br>By the fond carriage of such needless sums. |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | Believe me, Master Suresby, this is strange,<br>You being a man so settled in assurance<br>Will fall in that which you condemned in other. |
| MORE | [[Well]], Master Suresby, there’s your purse again,<br>And all your money. [[Fear]] nothing of More.<br>Wisdom still ( ) the door.<br>[Exeunt]<br><br>Sc. 3 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Earls of Shrewsbury and Surrey, Sir Thomas Palmer, and Sir Roger Cholmley |
| MORE | Mend this: |
| SHREWSBURY | My lord of Surrey, and Sir Thomas Palmer,<br><br>Might I with patience tempt your [[Grave\|grave]] advice?<br><br>I tell ye true, that in these dangerous times<br><br>I do not like this frowning vulgar brow.<br><br>My searching eye did never entertain<br><br>A more distracted countenance of grief<br><br>Than I have late observed<br><br>In the displeased commons of the [[City\|city]]. |
| SURREY | ’Tis strange, that from his princely clemency<br>So [[Well\|well]] a tempered [[Mercy\|mercy]] and a grace<br>To all the aliens in this fruitful land,<br>That this high-crested insolence should [[Spring\|spring]]<br>From them that breathe from his majestic bounty,<br>That, fattened with the traffic of our country,<br>Already leap into his subjects’ face. |
| PALMER | Yet Sherwin hindered to commence his suit<br>Against de Barde, by the Ambassador<br>By supplication made unto the [[King]];<br>Who, having first enticed away his wife<br>And got his plate, near worth four hundred pound,<br>To grieve some wronged citizens that found<br>This vile disgrace oft cast into their teeth,<br>Of late sues Sherwin, and arrested him<br>For money for the boarding of his wife. |
| SURREY | The more knave Barde, that, using Sherwin’s goods,<br>Doth ask him interest for the occupation.<br>I like not that, my lord of Shrewsbury.<br>He’s ill bestead that lends a [[Well\|well]]-paced [[Horse\|horse]]<br>Unto a man that will not find him meat. |
| CHOLMLEY | My lord of Surrey will be pleasant still. |
| PALMER | I being then employed by your honours<br>To stay the broil that fell about the same,<br>Where by [[Persuasion\|persuasion]] I enforced the wrongs<br>And urged the grief of the displeased [[City\|city]],<br>He answered me, and with a solemn oath,<br>That if he had the Mayor of [[London]]’s wife<br>He would keep her, in despite of any English man. |
| SURREY | ’Tis good, Sir Thomas, then, for you and me<br>Your wife is dead, and I a bachelor.<br>If no man can possess his wife [[Alone\|alone]],<br>I am glad, Sir Thomas Palmer, I have none. |
| CHOLMLEY | If a take my wife, a shall find her meat. |
| SURREY | And reason good, Sir Robert Cholmley, too:<br>If these hot Frenchmen needsly will have sport,<br>They should in kindness yet defray the charge.<br>’Tis hard when men possess our wives in quiet,<br>And yet leave us in to discharge their diet. |
| SHREWSBURY | My lord, our caters shall not use the market<br>For our provision but some stranger Lombard now<br>Will take the victuals from him he hath bought.<br>A carpenter, as I was late informed,<br>Who having bought a pair of doves in Cheap,<br>Immediately a Frenchman Lombard took them from him,<br>And beat the poor man for resisting him;<br>And when the fellow did complain his wrongs<br>He was severely punished for his labour. |
| SURREY | But if the English [[Blood\|blood]] be once but up,<br><br>As I perceive their hearts already full,<br><br>I [[Fear\|fear]] me much, before their spleens be cooled,<br><br>Some of these saucy aliens for their [[Pride\|pride]] 60<br><br>Will pay for’t soundly, wheresoe’er it lights.<br><br>This tide of rage that with the eddy strives,<br><br>I [[Fear\|fear]] me much, will drown too many lives. |
| CHOLMLEY | Now afore God, your honours, pardon me.<br><br>Men of your place and [[Greatness\|greatness]] are to blame- 65<br><br>I tell ye true, my lords-in that his majesty<br><br>Is not informed of this base abuse,<br><br>And daily wrongs are offered to his subjects;<br><br>For if he were, I know his gracious wisdom<br><br>Would soon redress it. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Messenger |
| CHOLMLEY | SfIREWSBURY<br>Sirrah, what news? |
| CHOLMLEY | None good, I [[Fear\|fear]]. |
| MESSENGER | My lord, ill news; and worse, I [[Fear\|fear]], will follow<br><br>If speedily it be not looked unto.<br><br>The [[City\|city]] is in an uproar, and the Mayor<br><br>Is threatened if he come out of his [[house]].<br><br>A number, poor artifices<br><br>[CHOLMLEY]<br>’Twas to be feared what this would come unto.<br>This follows on the Doctor’s publishing<br>The bill of wrongs in public at the Spital. |
| SHREWSBURY | That Doctor Beal may chance beshrew himself<br>For reading of the bill. |
| PALMER | Let us go gather forces to the Mayor<br>For quick suppressing this rebellious rout. |
| SURREY | Now I bethink myself of Master More,<br>One of the sheriffs, a wise and learned gentleman,<br>And in especial favour with the people.<br>He, backed with other [[Grave\|grave]] and sober men,<br>May by his gentle and persuasive speech<br>Perhaps prevail more than we can with power. |
| SHREWSBURY | Believe me but your [[Honour\|honour]] [[Well\|well]] advises.<br>Let us make haste, or I do greatly [[Fear\|fear]]<br>Some to their graves this morning’s work will bear. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |
| SHREWSBURY | Sc. 4 Enter [[Lincoln]], Betts, Williamson, Doll. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Lincoln]], [George] Betts, [Clown Betts,] Williamson, Sherwin, and other, armed; Doll in a shirt of mail, a headpiece, [[Sword\|sword]] and buckler; a crew attending |
| SHREWSBURY | [Original Text (Munday)]<br>[Addition Il (Heywood)] |
| CLOWN BETTS | Come, come, we’ll tickle their turnips, we’ll butter their boxes! Shall strangers rule the roast? Yes, but we’ll baste the roast. Come, come, aflaunt, aflaunt! |
| GEORGE BETTS | Brother, give place, and hear John [[Lincoln]] speak. |
| CLOWN BETTS | Ay, [[Lincoln]], my leader,<br>And Doll, my true breeder,<br>With the rest of our crew<br>Shall ran-tan-tarra-ran.<br>Do all they what they can,<br>Shall we be bobbed, braved?—No!<br>Shall we be held under?—No)<br>We are free born<br>And do take scorn<br>To be used so. |
| DOLL | Peace there, I say! Hear Captain [[Lincoln]] speak.<br>Keep [[Silence\|silence]] till we know his mind at large. |
| CLOWN BETTS | [to [[Lincoln]]] Then largely deliver. Speak, bully, and he that presumes to interrupt thee in thy oration, this for him! |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | Then gallant bloods, you whose free souls do scorn<br>To bear the enforced wrongs of aliens,<br>Add rage to resolution. [[Fire]] the houses<br>Of these audacious strangers. This is St Martin’s,<br>And yonder dwells Meautis, a wealthy Picardy,<br>At the Green Gate;<br>De Barde, Peter van Hollak, Adrian Martin,<br>With many more outlandish fugitives.<br>Shall these enjoy more privilege than we<br>In our own country? Let’s then become their slaves.<br>Since justice keeps not them in greater awe,<br>We’ll be ourselves rough ministers at law. |
| CLOWN BETTS | Use no more swords,<br>Nor no more words,<br>But [[Fire\|fire]] the houses,<br>Brave Captain Courageous,<br>[[Fire]] me their houses. |
| DOLL | Ay, for we may as [[Well\|well]] make bonfires on May Day as at Midsummer. We’ll alter the day in the calendar, and set it down in flaming letters. |
| SHERWIN | Stay, no, that would much endanger the whole [[City\|city]],<br>Whereto I would not the least prejudice. |
| DOLL | No, nor I neither: so may mine own [[house]] be burned for company. I’ll tell ye what: we’ll drag the strangers into Moorfields, and there bumbaste them till they stink again. |
| CLOWN BETTS | And that’s soon done, for they smell for [[Fear\|fear]] already. |
| GEORGE BETTS | Let some of us enter the strangers’ houses,<br>And, if we find them there, then bring them forth. |
| DOLL | But if ye bring them forth ere ye find them, I’ll ne’er allow of that. so |
| CLOWN BETTS | Now Mars for thy [[Honour\|honour]],<br>Dutch or French,<br>So it be a wench,<br>I’ll upon her.<br>[Exeunt Sherwin, Clown Betts, and others] |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] WILLIAMSON | Now, lads, how shall we labour in our safety?<br>I hear the Mayor hath gathered men in arms,<br>And that Sheriff More an hour ago received<br>Some of the Privy Council in at Ludgate.<br>Force now must make our peace, or else we fall.<br>’Twill soon be known we are the principal. |
| DOLL | And what of that? If thou beest afraid, husband, go [[Home\|home]] again and hide thy head, for, by the Lord, I’ll have a little sport now we are at it. |
| GEORGE BETTS | Let’s stand upon our swords, and if they come<br>Receive them as they were our enemies. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sherwin, [Clown Betts,] and the rest |
| CLOWN BETTS | A purchase, a purchase! We have found, we ha’ found— |
| DOLL | What? |
| CLOWN BETTS | Nothing. Not a French Fleming nor a Fleming French to be found, but all fled, in plain English. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] to Sherwin |
| CLOWN BETTS | How now, have you found any? |
| SHERWIN | No, not one, they’re all fled. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | Then [[Fire\|fire]] the houses, that, the Mayor being busy<br>About the quenching of them, we may scape.<br>Burn down their kennels! Let us straight away,<br>Lest this day prove to us an ill May Day. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but Clown |
| CLOWN BETTS | [[Fire]], [[Fire\|fire]]! I’ll be the first.<br>If hanging come, ’tis welcome; that’s the worst. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| CLOWN BETTS | [Addition II (Heywood)]<br>[Addition II (playhouse scribe)]<br><br>Sc. 5 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter at one door [[Sir Thomas More]] and [[Lord Mayor]]; at another door Sir John Munday, hurt |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | What, Sir John Munday, are you hurt? |
| SIR JOHN | A little knock, my lord. There was even now<br><br>A sort of prentices playing at cudgels.<br><br>I did command them to their masters’ houses,<br><br>But one of them, backed by the other crew,<br><br>Wounded me in the forehead with his cudgel;<br><br>And now, I [[Fear\|fear]] me, they are gone to join<br><br>With [[Lincoln]], Sherwin, and their dangerous train. |
| MORE | The captains of this insurrection<br>Have ta‘en themselves to arms, and came but now<br>To both the Counters, where they have released<br>Sundry indebted prisoners, and from thence<br>I hear that they are gone into St Martin’s,<br>Where they intend to offer violence<br>To the amazed Lombards. Therefore, my lord,<br>If we expect the safety of the [[City\|city]],<br>’Tis [[Time\|time]] that force or parley do encounter<br>With these displeased men. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Messenger |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | How now, what news? |
| MESSENGER | My lord, the rebels have broke open Newgate,<br>From whence they have delivered many prisoners, zo<br>Both felons and notorious murderers<br>That desperately cleave to their lawless train. |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | Up with the drawbridge! Gather some forces<br>To Cornhill and Cheapside. And, gentlemen,<br>If diligence be used on every side,<br>A quiet ebb will follow this rough tide. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Shrewsbury, Surrey, Palmer; Cholmley |
| SHREWSBURY | [[Lord Mayor]], his majesty, receiving notice<br>Of this most dangerous insurrection,<br>Hath sent my lord of Surrey and myself,<br>Sir Thomas Palmer, and our followers<br>To add unto your forces our best means<br>For pacifying of this mutiny.<br>In God’s name, then, set on with happy [[Speed\|speed]].<br>The [[King]] laments if one true subject bleed. |
| SURREY | I hear they mean to [[Fire\|fire]] the Lombards’ houses.<br>O power, what art thou in a madman’s eyes!<br>Thou mak’st the plodding idiot bloody-wise. |
| MORE | My lords, I [[Doubt\|doubt]] not but we shall appease<br>With a calm breath this flux of discontent. |
| PALMER | To call them to a parley questionless<br>May fall out good. ’Tis [[Well\|well]] said, Master More. |
| MORE | Let’s to these simple men, for many sweat<br>Under this act that knows not the law’s debt<br>Which hangs upon their lives. For silly men<br><br>Plod on they know not how; like a [[Fool\|fool]]’s pen<br><br>That, ending, shows not any sentence writ<br><br>Linked but to common reason or slightest wit.<br><br>These follow for no harm, but yet incur<br>[[Self]] penalty with those that raised this stir.<br>I’ God’s name on, to calm our private foes<br>With breath of gravity, not dangerous blows. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |
| MORE | Sc. 6 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Lincoln]], Doll, Clown [Betts,] George Betts, Williamson, [Sherwin,] others: [Citizens,] [armed] |
| MORE | [Addition II (playhouse scribe)]<br>[Addition II (Shakespeare)] |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | Peace, hear me! He that will not see a red herring at a Harry groat, butter at eleven pence a pound, meal at nine shillings a bushel, and beef at four nobles a stone, list to me. |
| OTHER GEORGE BETTS | It will come to that pass if strangers be suffered. Mark him. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | Our country is a great eating country; argo they eat more in our country than they do in their own. |
| OTHER CLOWN BETTS | By a halfpenny loaf a day, troy weight. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | They bring in strange roots, which is merely to the undoing of poor prentices. For what’s a sorry parsnip to a good heart? |
| OTHER WILLIAMSON | Trash, trash. They breed sore eyes, and ’tis enough to infect the [[City\|city]] with the palsy. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | Nay, it has infected it with the palsy, for these bastards of dung—as you know, they grow in dung—have infected us, and it is our infection will make the [[City\|city]] shake. Which partly comes through the eating of parsnips. |
| CLOWN BETTS | True, and pumpkins together. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Sergeant-at-arms |
| SERGEANT | What say ye to the [[Mercy\|mercy]] of the [[King]]?<br>Do you refuse it? |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | You would have us upon th’ hip, would you? No, marry, do we not. We accept of the [[King]]’s [[Mercy\|mercy]], but we will show no [[Mercy\|mercy]] upon the strangers. |
| SERGEANT | You are the simplest things that ever stood In such a question. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | How say you now, prentices? Prentices ‘simple’? Down with him! |
| ALL CITIZENS | Prentices simple? Prentices simple? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Lord Mayor]], Surrey, Shrewsbury, [More, Palmer] |
| MAYOR | Hold, in the [[King]]’s name, hold! |
| SURREY | Friends, masters, countrymen— |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | Peace ho, peace! I charge you keep the peace. |
| SHREWSBURY | My masters, countrymen— |
| WILLIAMSON | The noble Earl of Shrewsbury! Let’s hear him. |
| GEORGE BETTS | We’ll hear the [[Earl of Surrey]]. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | The Earl of Shrewsbury! |
| GEORGE BEETS | We’ll hear both. |
| ALL CITIZENS | Both, both, both, both! |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | Peace, I say, peace! Are you men of wisdom, or what are you? |
| SURREY | What you will have them, but not men of wisdom. |
| SOME CITIZENS | We’ll not hear my lord of Surrey. |
| OTHER CITIZENS | No, no, no, no, no! Shrewsbury, Shrewsbury! |
| MORE | Whiles they are o’er the bank of their obedience<br>Thus will they bear down all things. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | Sheriff More speaks. Shall we hear Sheriff More speak? |
| DOLL | Let’s hear him. A keeps a plentiful shrievaltry, and a made my brother, [[arthur\|Arthur]] Watchins, Sergeant Safe’s yeoman. Let’s hear Sheriff More! |
| ALL CITIZENS | Sheriff More, More, More, Sheriff More! |
| MORE | Even by the rule you have among yourselves,<br>Command still audience. |
| SOME CITIZENS | Surrey, Surrey! |
| OTHER CITIZENS | More, More! |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | and GEORGE BETTS Peace, peace, [[Silence\|silence]], peace! |
| MORE | You that have voice and credit with the number,<br>Command them to a stillness. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | A plague on them, they will not hold their peace. The devil cannot rule them. |
| MORE | Then what a rough and riotous charge have you<br>To lead those that the devil cannot rule.—<br>Good masters, hear me speak. |
| DOLL | Ay, by th’ mass will we, More. Thou’rt a good housekeeper, and I thank thy good worship for my brother [[arthur\|Arthur]] Watchins. |
| ALL THE OTHER CITIZENS | Peace, peace! |
| MORE | Look what you do offend you cry upon;<br>That is, the peace. Not one of you here present,<br>Had there such fellows lived when you were babes<br>That could have topped the peace as now you would,<br>The peace wherein you have till now grown up<br>Had been ta’en from you, and the bloody times<br>Could not have brought you to the state of men.<br>Alas, poor things! What is it you have got<br>Although we grant you get the thing you seek? |
| GEORGE BETTS | Marry, the removing of the strangers, which cannot choose but much advantage the poor handicrafts of the [[City\|city]]. |
| MORE | Grant them removed, and grant that this your noise<br>Hath chid down all the majesty of England.<br>Imagine that you see the wretched strangers,<br>Their babies at their backs, with their poor luggage,<br>Plodding to th’ ports and coasts for transportation,<br>And that you sit as kings in your desires,<br>Authority quite silenced by your brawl,<br>And you in ruff of your opinions clothed:<br>What had you got? I’ll tell you: you had taught<br>How insolence and strong hand should prevail,<br>How order should be quelled. And by this pattern<br>Not one of you should live an aged man;<br>For other ruffians, as their fancies wrought,<br>With selfsame hand, [[Self\|self]] reasons, and [[Self\|self]] right,<br>Would shark on you, and men, like ravenous fishes,<br>Would feed on one another. |
| DOLL | Before God, that’s as true as the gospel. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | Nay, this’ a sound fellow, I tell you. Let’s mark him. |
| MORE | Let me set up before your thoughts, good friends,<br>One supposition, which if you will mark<br>You shall perceive how horrible a shape<br>Your innovation bears. First, ‘tis a sin<br>Which oft th’apostle did forewarn us of,<br>Urging obedience to authority;<br>And ‘twere no error if I told you all<br>You were in arms ’gainst God. |
| ALL CITIZENS | Marry, God forbid thatl |
| MORE | Nay, certainly you are. no<br>For to the [[King\|king]] God hath His office lent<br>Of dread, of justice, power, and command;<br>Hath bid him rule, and willed you to obey.<br>And, to add ampler majesty to this,<br>He hath not only lent the [[King\|king]] His figure,<br>His throne and [[Sword\|sword]], but given him His own name:<br>Calls him a god on earth. What do you, then,<br>Rising ‘gainst him that God Himself installs,<br>But rise ’gainst God? What do you to your souls<br>In doing this? O, desperate as you are,<br>Wash your foul minds with [[Tears\|tears]], and those same hands<br>That you, like rebels, lift against the peace,<br>Lift up for peace; and your unreverent knees,<br>Make them your feet. To kneel to be forgiven<br>Is safer wars than ever you can make<br><br>Whose discipline is riot.<br><br>In, in, to your obedience! Why, even your hurly<br><br>Cannot proceed but by obedience.<br><br>Tell me but this: What [[Rebel\|rebel]] captain,<br>As mutinies are incident, by his name<br>Can still the rout? Who will obey a traitor?<br>Or how can [[Well\|well]] that proclamation sound<br>When there is no addition but ‘a [[Rebel\|rebel]]’<br>To qualify a [[Rebel\|rebel]]? You’ll put down strangers,<br>Kill them, cut their throats, possess their houses,<br>And lead the majesty of law in lyam<br>To slip him like a hound. Alas, alas! Say now the [[King]],<br>As he is clement if th’offender mourn,<br>Should so much come too short of your great trespass<br>As but to banish you: whither would you go?<br>What country, by the [[Nature\|nature]] of your error,<br>Should give you harbour? Go you to France or<br>Flanders,<br>To any German province, Spain or Portugal,<br>Nay, anywhere that not adheres to England:<br>Why, you must needs be strangers. Would you be<br>pleased<br>To find a nation of such barbarous temper<br>That, breaking out in hideous violence,<br>Would not afford you an abode on earth,<br>Whet their detested knives against your throats,<br>Spurn you like [[dogs]], and like as if that God<br>Owed not nor made not you, nor that the elements<br>Were not all appropriate to your comforts,<br>But chartered unto them? What would you think<br>To be thus used? This is the strangers’ case,<br>And this your mountainish inhumanity. |
| ALL CITIZENS | [[Faith]], a says true. Let us do as we may be done by. |
| ALL CITIZENS [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | We’ll be ruled by you, Master More, if you’ll stand our friend to procure our pardon. |
| MORE | Submit you to these noble gentlemen,<br>Entreat their mediation to the [[King]],<br>Give up yourself to form, obey the magistrate,<br>And there’s no [[Doubt\|doubt]] but [[Mercy\|mercy]] may be found<br>If you so seek it.<br><br>[Addition 11 (Shakespeare)]<br>[Original Text (Munday)] |
| ALL CITIZENS | We yield, and [[Desire\|desire]] his highness’ [[Mercy\|mercy]]. They lay by their weapons |
| MORE | No [[Doubt\|doubt]] his majesty will grant it you.<br>But you must yield to go to several prisons<br>Till that his highness’ will be further known. |
| ALL CITIZENS | Most willingly, whither you will have us. |
| SHREWSBURY | [[Lord Mayor]], let them be sent to several prisons,<br>And there, in any case, be [[Well\|well]] entreated.<br>My lord of Surrey, please you to take [[Horse\|horse]]<br>And ride to Cheapside, where the aldermen<br>Are with their several companies in arms.<br>Will them to go unto their several wards,<br>Both for the stay of further mutiny<br>And for the apprehending of such persons<br>As shall contend. |
| SURREY | I go, my noble lord. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| SHREWSBURY | We’ll straight go tell his highness these good news.<br>Withal, Sheriff More, I’ll tell him how your breath<br>Hath ransomed many a subject from sad [[Death\|death]]. Exit |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | [[Lincoln]] and Sherwin, you shall both to Newgate,<br>The rest unto the Counters. |
| PALMER | Go, guard them hence. A little breath [[Well\|well]] spent<br>Cheats expectation in his fair’st event. |
| DOLL | [[Well]], Sheriff More, thou hast done more with thy good words than all they could with their weapons. Give me thy hand. Keep thy promise now for the [[King]]’s pardon, or, by the Lord, I’ll call thee a plain cony-catcher. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | Farewell, Sheriff More. And as we yield by thee<br>So make our peace; then thou deal’st honestly. |
| CLOWN BETTS | Ay, and save us from the gallows, else a deals [[Double\|double]] honestly.<br>[The Citizens] are led away |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | Master Sheriff More, you have preserved the [[City\|city]]<br>From a most dangerous fierce commotion.<br>For if this limb of riot here in St Martin’s<br>Had joined with other branches of the [[City\|city]]<br>That did begin to kindle, ’twould have bred<br>Great rage. That rage much murder would have fed. |
| PALMER | Not steel but eloquence hath wrought this good.<br><br>You have redeemed us from much threatened [[Blood\|blood]]. |
| MORE | My lord, and brethren, what I here have spoke<br>My country’s [[Love\|love]] and, next, the [[City\|city]]’s care<br>Enjoined me to; which since it thus prevails,<br>Think God hath made weak More His instrument<br>To thwart sedition’s violent intent.<br>I think ’twere best, my lord, some two hours hence<br>We meet at the Guildhall, and there determine<br>That thorough every ward the watch be clad<br>In armour. But especially provide<br>That at the [[City\|city]] gates selected men,<br>Substantial citizens, do ward tonight,<br>For [[Fear\|fear]] of further mischief. |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | It shall be so. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Shrewsbury |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | But yon, methinks, my lord of Shrewsbury. |
| SHREWSBURY | My lord, his majesty sends loving thanks<br>To you, your brethren, and his faithful subjects<br>Your careful citizens. But Master More, to you<br>A rougher yet as kind a salutation.<br>Your name is yet too short. Nay, you must kneel.<br>A knight’s creation is this knightly steel. |
| *(didascalia)* | More kneels |
| SHREWSBURY | Rise up [[Sir Thomas More]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | He knights More |
| *(didascalia)* | MORE rising |
| SHREWSBURY | I thank his highness for thus honouring me. |
| SHREWSBURY | This is but first taste of his princely favour,<br>For it hath pleased his high majesty,<br>Noting your wisdom and deserving merit,<br>To put this staff of [[Honour\|honour]] in your hand,<br>For he hath chose you of his Privy Council. |
| *(didascalia)* | He gives More a staff of office |
| MORE | My lord, for to deny my sovereign’s bounty<br>Were to drop precious stones into the heaps<br>Whence first they came.<br>To urge my imperfections in excuse<br>Were all as stale as custom. No, my lord,<br>My service is my [[King\|king]]’s. Good reason why,<br>Since life or [[Death\|death]] hangs on our sovereign’s eye. |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | His majesty hath honoured much the [[City\|city]]<br>In this his princely choice. |
| MORE | My lord and brethren,<br>Though I depart for [[Court\|court]], my [[Love\|love]] shall rest<br>〈 〉<br>I now must [[Sleep\|sleep]] in [[Court\|court]], sound sleeps forbear.<br>The chamberlain to state is public care.<br>Yet in this rising of my private [[Blood\|blood]]<br>My studious thoughts shall tend the [[City\|city]]’s good. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Croft |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Crofts |
| SHREWSBURY | How now, Crofts? What news? |
| CROFTS | My lord, his highness sends express command<br>That a record be entered of this riot,<br>And that the chief and capital offenders<br>Be thereon straight arraigned; for himself intends<br>To sit in person on the rest tomorrow<br>At Westminster. |
| SHREWSBURY | [[Lord Mayor]], you hear your charge.<br>Come, good [[Sir Thomas More]], to [[Court\|court]] let’s hie.<br>You are th’appeaser of this mutiny. |
| MORE | My lord, farewell. New days begets new tides.<br>Life whirls ’bout fate, then to a [[Grave\|grave]] it slides. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |
| MORE | Sc. 7 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Master Sheriff, and meet a Messenger |
| SHERIFF | Messenger, what news? |
| MESSENGER | Is execution yet performed? |
| SHERIFF | Not yet. The carts stand ready at the stairs,<br>And they shall presently away to Tyburn. |
| MESSENGER | Stay, Master Sheriff. It is the Council’s pleasure,<br>For more example in so bad a case,<br>A gibbet be erected in Cheapside<br>Hard by the Standard, whither you must bring<br>[[Lincoln]], and those that were the chief with him,<br>To suffer [[Death\|death]], and that immediately. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Officers |
| SHERIFF | It shall be done, sir. Exit Messenger<br>Officers, be speedy.<br>Call for a gibbet, see it be erected.<br>Others make haste to Newgate; bid them bring<br>The prisoners hither, for they here must die.<br>Away, I say, and see no [[Time\|time]] be slacked. |
| OFFICERS | We go, sir. |
| SHERIFF | That’s [[Well\|well]] said, fellows. Now you do your duty. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt some Officers severally. Others set up the gibbet |
| SHERIFF | God for his pity help these troublous times!<br><br>The street’s stopped up with gazing multitudes.<br><br>Command our armed officers with halberds<br><br>Make way for entrance of the prisoners.<br><br>Let proclamation once again be made<br><br>That every householder, on [[Pain\|pain]] of [[Death\|death]],<br><br>Keep in his prentices, and every man<br><br>Stand with a weapon ready at his door,<br><br>As he will answer to the contrary. |
| FIRST OFFICER | I’ll see it done, sir. Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter another Offices |
| SHERIFF | Bring them away to execution.<br><br>The writ is come above two hours since.<br><br>The [[City\|city]] will be fined for this neglect. |
| SECOND OFFICER | There’s such a press and multitude at Newgate<br>They cannot bring the carts unto the stairs<br>To take the prisoners in. |
| SHERIFF | Then let them come on foot.<br>We may not dally [[Time\|time]] with great command. |
| SECOND OFFICER | Some of the Bench, sir, think it very fit<br>That stay be made, and give it out abroad<br>The execution is deferred till morning;<br>And when the streets shall be a little cleared<br>To chain them up, and suddenly dispatch it. |
| *(didascalia)* | The prisoners are brought in, amongst them [[Lincoln]], |
| *(didascalia)* | Doll, Williamson, Clown Betts, and Sherwin, [[Well\|well]] |
| *(didascalia)* | guarded, and the Executioner |
| SHERIFF | Stay, in meantime methinks they come along.<br>See, they are coming. So, ’tis very [[Well\|well]].<br>Bring [[Lincoln]] there the first unto the tree. |
| CLOWN BETTS | Ay, for I cry lag, sir. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | I knew the first, sir, did belong to me.<br>This the old proverb now complete doth make:<br>That ‘[[Lincoln]] should be hanged for [[London]]’s sake’. |
| *(didascalia)* | I’ God’s name, let’s to work. To Executioner Fellow, dispatch. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | He goes up<br>I was the foremost man in this rebellion,<br>And I the foremost that must die for it. |
| DOLL | Bravely, John [[Lincoln]], let thy [[Death\|death]] express<br>That, as thou lived‘st a man, thou died’st no less. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | Doll Williamson, thine eyes shall witness it.<br>Then to all you that come to view mine end<br>I must confess I had no ill intent<br>But against such as wronged us overmuch.<br>And now I can perceive it was not fit<br>That private men should carve out their redress<br>Which way they list. No, learn it now by me:<br>Obedience is the best in each degree.<br>And, asking [[Mercy\|mercy]] meekly of my [[King\|king]],<br>I patiently submit me to the law.<br>But God forgive them that were cause of it;<br>And, as a Christian, truly from my heart,<br>I likewise crave they would forgive me too,<br>〈 〉<br>That others by example of the same<br>Henceforth be warned to attempt the like<br>’Gainst any alien that repaireth hither.<br>Fare ye [[Well\|well]] all. The next [[Time\|time]] that we meet<br>I trust in heaven we shall each other greet. He leaps off |
| DOLL | Farewell, John [[Lincoln]]. Say all what they can,<br>Thou lived‘st a good fellow, and died’st an honest man. |
| CLOWN BETTS | Would I were so far on my [[Journey\|journey]]. The first stretch is the worst, methinks. |
| SHERIFF | Bring Williamson there forward. |
| DOLL | Good Master Sheriff, I have an earnest suit,<br>And, as you are a man, deny’t me not. |
| SHERIFF | Woman, what is it? Be it in my power,<br>Thou shalt obtain it. |
| DOLL | Let me die next, sir, that is all I crave.<br>You know not what a comfort you shall bring<br>To my poor heart to die before my husband. |
| SHERIFF | Bring her to [[Death\|death]]. She shall have her [[Desire\|desire]]. |
| CLOWN BETTS | Sir, and I have a suit to you too. |
| SHERIFF | What is it? |
| CLOWN BETTS | That, as you have hanged [[Lincoln]] first and will hang her next, so you will not hang me at all. |
| SHERIFF | Nay, you set ope the Counter gates, and you must hang chiefly. |
| CLOWN BETTS | [[Well]] then, so much for that! |
| *(didascalia)* | DOLL to Sherif |
| CLOWN BETTS | Sir, your free bounty much contents my mind.<br>Commend me to that good sheriff Master More,<br>And tell him had’t not been for his [[Persuasion\|persuasion]]<br>John [[Lincoln]] had not hung here as he does.<br>We would first have locked up in Leaden Hall,<br>And there been burned to ashes with the roof. |
| SHERIFF | Woman, what Master More did was a subject’s duty,<br>And hath so pleased our gracious lord the [[King]]<br>That he is hence removed to higher place<br>And made of Council to his majesty. |
| DOLL | [[Well]] is he worthy of it, by my troth:<br>An honest, wise, [[Well\|well]]-spoken gentleman.<br>Yet would I praise his honesty much more<br>If he had kept his word and saved our lives.<br>But let that pass. Men are but men, and so<br>Words are but words, and pays not what men owe.<br>Now, husband, since perhaps the world may say<br>That through my means thou com’st thus to thy end,<br>Here I begin this cup of [[Death\|death]] to thee,<br>Because thou shalt be sure to taste no worse<br>Than I have taken that must go before thee.<br>What though I be a woman? That’s no matter.<br>I do owe God a [[Death\|death]], and I must pay him.<br>Husband, give me thy hand. Be not dismayed.<br>This chore being chored, then all our debt is paid.<br>Only two little babes we leave behind us,<br>And all I can bequeath them at this [[Time\|time]]<br>Is but the [[Love\|love]] of some good honest friend<br>To bring them up in charitable sort.<br>What, masters?—He goes upright that never halts,<br>And they may live to mend their parents’ faults. |
| WILLIAMSON | Why, [[Well\|well]] said, wife. I‘[[Faith\|faith]], thou cheer’st my heart.<br>Give me thy hand. Let’s kiss, and so let’s part.<br>He kisses her on the ladder |
| DOLL | The next kiss, Williamson, shall be in heaven.<br>Now cheerly, lads! George Betts, a hand with thee. |
| *(didascalia)* | To Clown Betts And thine too, Ralph. And thine, good |
| DOLL | honest Sherwin.<br>Now let me tell the women of this town<br>No stranger yet brought Doll to lying down.<br>So long as I an Englishman can see,<br>Nor French nor Dutch shall get a kiss of me.<br>And when that I am dead, for me yet say<br>I died in scorn to be a stranger’s prey. |
| *(didascalia)* | A great shout and noise within |
| VOICES WITHIN | Pardon, pardon, pardon, pardon!<br>Room for the Earl of Surreyl Room there, room! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Surrey |
| SURREY | Save the man’s life, if it be possible! |
| SHERIFF | It is too late, my lord, he’s dead already. |
| SURREY | I tell ye, Master Sheriff, you are too forward<br>To make such haste with men unto their [[Death\|death]].<br>I think your pains will merit little thanks,<br>Since that his highness is so merciful<br>As not to spill the [[Blood\|blood]] of any subject. |
| SHERIFF | My noble lord, would we so much had known!<br>The Council’s warrant hastened our dispatch.<br>It had not else been done so suddenly. |
| SURREY | [[Sir Thomas More]] humbly upon his knee<br>Did beg the lives of all, since on his word<br>They did so gently yield. The [[King]] hath granted it,<br>And made him Lord High Chancellor of England,<br>According as he worthily deserves.<br>Since [[Lincoln]]’s life cannot be had again,<br>Then for the rest, from my dread sovereign’s lips,<br>I here pronounce free pardon for them all— |
| ALL | (flinging up caps)<br>God save the [[King]]! God save the [[King]],<br>My good Lord Chancellor and the Earl of Surreyl |
| DOLL | And Doll desires it from her very heart<br>More’s name may live for this right noble part;<br>And whensoe’er we talk of Ill May Day<br>Praise More, whose honest words our falls did stay. |
| SURREY | In [[Hope\|hope]] his highness’ clemency and [[Mercy\|mercy]],<br>Which in the arms of mild and meek compassion<br>Would rather clip you, as the loving [[Nurse\|nurse]]<br>Oft doth the wayward infant, than to leave you<br>To the sharp rod of justice; so to draw you<br>To shun such lewd assemblies as beget<br>Unlawful riots and such traitorous acts<br>That, striking with the hand of private hate,<br>Maim your dear country with a public wound.<br>O God, that [[Mercy\|mercy]], whose majestic brow<br><br>Should be unwrinkled, and that awe-full justice,<br><br>Which looketh through a veil of sufferance<br><br>Upon the frailty of the multitude,<br><br>Should with the clamours of outrageous wrongs<br><br>Be stirred and wakened thus to punishment!<br><br>But your deserved [[Death\|death]] he doth forgive.<br>Who gives you life, pray all he long may live. |
| ALL | God save the [[King]]! God save the [[King]],<br>My good Lord Chancellor, and the Earl of Surreyl |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |
| ALL | [Original Text (Munday)] |
| *(didascalia)* | [Addition III (playhouse scribe; attributed to Shakespeare)] |
| ALL | Sc. 8<br>A table being covered with a green carpet, a state cushion on it, and the purse and mace lying thereon, enter More |
| MORE | It is in heaven that I am thus and thus,<br>And that which we profanely term our fortunes<br>Is the provision of the power above,<br>Fitted and shaped just to that strength of [[Nature\|nature]]<br>Which we are born with. Good God, good God,<br>That I from such an humble bench of birth<br>Should step, as ’twere, up to my country’s head,<br>And give the law out there; I, in my father’s life,<br>To take prerogative and tithe of knees<br>From elder kinsmen, and him bind by my place<br>To give the smooth and dexter way to me<br>That owe it him by [[Nature\|nature]]: sure these things,<br>Not physicked by respect, might turn our [[Blood\|blood]]<br>To much corruption. But, More, the more thou<br>hast,<br>Either of [[Honour\|honour]], office, wealth, and calling,<br>Which might accite thee to embrace and hug them,<br>The more do thou in serpent’s natures think them,<br>[[Fear]] their gay skins with thought of their sharp state,<br>And let this be thy maxim: to be great<br>Is, when the thread of hazard is once spun,<br>A [[Bottom\|bottom]] great wound up, greatly undone.<br><br>[Addition III (playhouse scribe; attributed to Shakespeare)]<br>[addition IV (playhouse scribe; attributed to Dekker)] |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Sir Thomas More]]’s man Randall, attired like him |
| MORE | Come on, sir, are you ready? |
| RANDALL | Yes, my lord. I stand but on a few points. I shall have done presently. Before God, I have practised your lordship’s shift so [[Well\|well]] that I think I shall grow proud, my lord. |
| MORE | ‘Tis fit thou shouldst wax proud, or else thou’lt ne‘er<br>Be near allied to [[Greatness\|greatness]]. Observe me, sirrah.<br>The learned clerk Erasmus is arrived |
| *(didascalia)* | Within our English [[Court\|court]]. Last [[Night\|night]], I hear, |
| MORE | He feasted with our English honoured poet<br>The [[Earl of Surrey]], and I learned today<br>The famous clerk of Rotterdam will visit<br>[[Sir Thomas More]]. Therefore, sir, take my seat.<br>You are Lord Chancellor. Dress your behaviour<br>According to my carriage. But beware<br>You talk not overmuch, for ’twill betray thee.<br>Who prates not much seems wise, his wit few scan,<br>While the tongue blabs tales of the imperfect man.<br>I’ll see if great Erasmus can distinguish<br>Merit and outward ceremony. |
| RANDALL | If I do not deserve a share for playing of your lordship [[Well\|well]], let me be yeoman usher to your sumpter and be banished from wearing of a gold chain forever. |
| MORE | [[Well]], sir, I’ll hide our motion. Act my part<br>With a firm boldness, and thou winn’st my heart. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Sheriff, with Falkner (a ruffian) and Officers |
| MORE | How now, what’s the matter? |
| *(didascalia)* | FALKNER to Officers Tug me not; I’m no bear. ‘Sblood, if all the [[dogs]] in [[Paris]] [[Garden]] hung at my tail, I’d shake ’em off with this: that I’ll appear before no [[King\|king]] christened but my good Lord Chancellor. |
| SHERIFF | We’ll christen you, sirrah.—Bring him forward. |
| *(didascalia)* | MORE to Falkner How now, what tumults make you? |
| FALKNER | The azured heavens protect my noble Lord Chancellor! |
| *(didascalia)* | MORE to Sheriff What fellow’s this? |
| SHERIFF | A ruffian, my lord, that hath set half the [[City\|city]] in an uproar. |
| FALKNER | My lord— |
| SHERIFF | There was a fray in Paternoster Row, and because they would not be parted the street was choked up with carts. |
| FALKNER | My noble lord, Pannyer Alley’s throat was open. |
| MORE | Sirrah, hold your peace. |
| FALKNER | I’ll prove the street was not choked, but is as [[Well\|well]] as ever it was since it was a street. |
| SHERIFF | This fellow was a principal broacher of the broil— |
| FALKNER | ’Sblood, I broached none. It was broached and half run out before I had a lick at it. |
| SHERIFF | And would be brought before no justice but your [[Honour\|honour]]. |
| FALKNER | ! I am hauled, my noble lord. |
| *(didascalia)* | MORE to Sheriff |
| FALKNER | No ear to choose for every trivial noise<br>But mine, and in so full a [[Time\|time]]? Away.<br>You wrong me, Master Sheriff. Dispose of him<br>At your own pleasure. Send the knave to Newgate. |
| FALKNER | To Newgate? ’Sblood, [[Sir Thomas More]], I appeal, I appeal: from Newgate to any of the two worshipful Counters. |
| MORE | Fellow, whose man are you that are thus lusty? |
| FALKNER | My name’s Jack Falkner. I serve, next under God and my prince, Master Morris, secretary to my lord of Winchester. |
| MORE | A fellow of your hair is very fit<br>To be a secretary’s follower! |
| FALKNER | I [[Hope\|hope]] so, my lord. The fray was between the Bishop’s men of Ely and Winchester, and I could not in [[Honour\|honour]] but part them. I thought it stood not with my reputation and degree to come to my questions and answers before a [[City\|city]] justice. I knew I should to the pot. |
| MORE | Thou hast been there, it seems, too late already. |
| FALKNER | I know your [[Honour\|honour]] is wise, and so forth, and I [[Desire\|desire]] to be only catechized or examined by you, my noble Lord Chancellor. |
| MORE | Sirrah, sirrah, you are a busy dangerous ruffian. FALKNER Ruffian? |
| MORE | How long have you worn this hair? |
| FALKNER | I have worn this hair ever since I was born. |
| MORE | You know that’s not my question: but how long<br>Hath this shag fleece hung dangling on thy head? |
| FALKNER | How long, my lord? Why, sometimes thus long, sometimes lower, as the Fates and humours please. |
| MORE | So quick, sir, with me, ha? I see, good fellow,<br>Thou lovest plain dealing. Sirrah, tell me now<br>When were you last at barber’s? How long [[Time\|time]]<br>Have you upon your head worn this shag hair? |
| FALKNER | My lord, Jack Falkner tells no Aesop’s fables. Troth, I was not at barber’s this three years. I have not been cut, nor will not be cut, upon a foolish vow which, as the Destinies shall direct, I am sworn to keep. |
| MORE | When comes that vow out? |
| FALKNER | Why, when the humours are purged; not these three years. |
| MORE | Vows are recorded in the [[Court\|court]] of heaven,<br>For they are holy acts. Young man, I charge thee<br>And do advise thee start not from that vow.<br>And for I will be sure thou shalt not shear,<br>Besides because it is an odious sight<br>To see a man thus hairy, thou shalt lie<br>In Newgate till thy vow and thy three years<br>Be full expired.—Away with him. |
| FALKNER | My lord— |
| MORE | Cut off this fleece and lie there but a month. |
| FALKNER | I’ll not lose a hair to be Lord Chancellor of Europe. |
| MORE | To Newgate then. Sirrah, great sins are bred<br>In all that [[Body\|body]] where there’s a foul head. |
| *(didascalia)* | Away with him. Exeunt all but Randall |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Surrey, Erasmus, and attendants |
| SURREY | Now, great Erasmus, you approach the presence<br>Of a most worthy learned gentleman.<br>This little isle holds not a truer friend<br>Unto the arts; nor doth his [[Greatness\|greatness]] add<br>A feigned flourish to his worthy parts.<br>He’s great in study: that’s the statist’s grace<br>That gains more reverence than the outward place. |
| ERASMUS | Report, my lord, hath crossed the narrow seas,<br>And to the several parts of Christendom<br>Hath borne the fame of your Lord Chancellor.<br>I long to see him whom with loving thoughts<br>I in my study oft have visited.<br>Is that [[Sir Thomas More]]? |
| SURREY | It is, Erasmus.<br>Now shall you view the honourablest scholar,<br>The most religious politician,<br>The worthiest counsellor, that tends our state.<br>That study is the general watch of England.<br>In it, the Prince’s safety and the peace<br>That shines upon our commonwealth are forged<br>By loyal industry. |
| ERASMUS | I [[Doubt\|doubt]] him not<br>To be as near the life of excellence<br>As you proclaim him, when his meanest servants<br>Are of some weight. You saw, my lord, his porter<br>Give entertainment to us at the gate<br>In Latin good phrase. What’s the master, then,<br>When such good parts shine in his meanest men? |
| SURREY | His lordship hath some weighty business,<br>For, see, as yet he takes no notice of us. |
| ERASMUS | I think ’twere best I did my duty to him<br>In a short Latin speech. |
| *(didascalia)* | He takes off his hat and addresses Randall |
| ERASMUS | Qui in celeberrima patria natus est et gloriosa plus habet<br><br>negotii ut in lucem veniat quam qui— |
| RANDALL | I prithee, good Erasmus, be covered. I have forsworn speaking of Latin else, as I am true councillor, I’d tickle you with a speech. Nay, sit, Erasmus. Sit, good my lord of Surrey. I’ll make my lady come to you anon, if she will, and give you entertainment. |
| ERASMUS | Is this [[Sir Thomas More]]? |
| SURREY | O good Erasmus,<br>You must conceive his vein. He’s ever furnished<br>With these conceits. |
| RANDALL | Yes, [[Faith\|faith]], my learned poet doth not lie for that matter. I am neither more nor less than merry Sir Thomas always. Wilt’ sup with me? By God, I [[Love\|love]] a parlous wise fellow that smells of a politician better than a long progress. Enter [[Sir Thomas More]] |
| SURREY | We are deluded. This is not his lordship. |
| RANDALL | I pray you, Erasmus, how long will the Holland [[cheese]] in your country keep without maggots? |
| MORE | [[Fool]], painted barbarism, retire thyself<br>Into thy first creation. Thus you see,<br>My loving learned friends, how far respect<br>Waits often on the ceremonious train<br>Of base illiterate wealth, whilst men of schools,<br>Shrouded in [[Poverty\|poverty]], are counted fools.<br>Pardon, thou reverend German, I have mixed<br>So slight a jest to the fair entertainment<br>Of thy most worthy [[Self\|self]]. For know, Erasmus,<br>Mirth wrinkles up my face, and I still crave<br>When that forsakes me I may hug my [[Grave\|grave]].<br>Aut tu Erasmus aut diabolus. |
| ERASMUS | Your [[Honour\|honour]]’s merry [[Humour\|humour]] is best physic<br>Unto your able [[Body\|body]], for we learn<br>Where [[Melancholy\|melancholy]] chokes the passages<br>Of [[Blood\|blood]] and breath, the erected spirit still<br>Lengthens our days with sportful exercise.<br>Study should be the saddest [[Time\|time]] of life;<br>The rest a sport exempt from thought of strife. |
| MORE | Erasmus preacheth gospel against physic.—<br>My noble poet— |
| SURREY | O my lord, you tax me<br>In that word ‘poet’ of much idleness.<br>It is a study that makes poor our fate.<br>Poets were ever thought unfit for state. |
| MORE | O, give not up fair poesy, sweet lord,<br>To such contempt. That I may speak my heart,<br>It is the sweetest heraldry of art<br>That sets a difference ’tween the tough, sharp holly<br>And tender bay tree. |
| SURREY | Yet, my lord,<br>It is become the very lag i’ number<br>To all mechanic sciences. |
| MORE | Why I’ll show the reason<br>This is no age for poets. They should sing<br>To the loud canon heroica facta:<br><br>Qui faciunt reges heroica carmina laudant;<br>And, as great subjects of their pen [[Decay\|decay]],<br>Even so, unphysicked, they do melt away. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Master Morris |
| MORE | Come, will your lordship in? My dear Erasmus—<br>I’ll hear you, Master Morris, presently.— |
| *(didascalia)* | To Erasmus My lord, I make you master of my [[house]]. |
| MORE | We’ll banquet here with fresh and staid delights.<br>The Muses’ music here shall cheer our spirits.<br>The cates must be but mean where scholars sit;<br>For they’re made all with courses of neat wit. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Surrey, Erasmus, and attendants |
| MORE | How now, Master Morris? |
| MORRIS | I am a suitor to your lordship in behalf of a servant of mine. |
| MORE | The fellow with long hair, good Master Morris?<br>Come to me three years hence, and then I’ll hear you. |
| MORRIS | I understand your [[Honour\|honour]]; but the foolish knave has submitted himself to the [[Mercy\|mercy]] of a barber, and is without, ready to make a new vow before your lordship hereafter to live civil. |
| MORE | Nay then, let’s talk with him; pray call him in. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Falkner and Officers |
| FALKNER | Bless your [[Honour\|honour]]: a new man, my lord. |
| MORE | Why sure this’ not he. |
| FALKNER | An your lordship will, the barber shall give you a sample of my head. I am he, in [[Faith\|faith]], my lord, I am ipse. |
| MORE | Why, now thy face is like an honest man’s.<br>Thou hast played [[Well\|well]] at this new-cut and won. |
| FALKNER | No, my lord, lost all that ever God sent me. |
| MORE | God sent thee into the world as thou art now, with a short hair. How quickly are three years run out in Newgatel |
| FALKNER | I think so, my lord, for there was but a hair’s length between my going thither and so long [[Time\|time]]. |
| MORE | Because I see some grace in thee, go free.— |
| *(didascalia)* | Discharge him, fellows. Exeunt Officers |
| MORE | Farewell, Master Morris.<br>Thy head is for thy shoulders now more fit: |
| *(didascalia)* | Thou hast less hair upon it, but more wit. exit |
| MORRIS | Did not I tell thee always of these locks? |
| FALKNER | An the locks were on again, all the goldsmiths in Cheapside should not pick them open. ’Sheart, if my hair stand not on end when I look for my face in a glass, I am a potecat.—Here’s a lousy jest.—But if I notch not that rogue Tom Barber that makes me look thus like a Brownist, hang me. I’ll be worse to the nittical knave than ten tooth-drawings. Here’s a head with a pox!<br><br>[Addition IV (playhouse scribe; attributed to Dekker)]<br>[Addition IV (Dekker)] |
| MORRIS | What ail’st thou? Art thou mad now? |
| FALKNER | Mad now? Nails, if loss of hair cannot mad a man—what can? I am deposed: my [[Crown\|crown]] is taken from me. More had been better a’ scoured Moorditch than a’ notched me thus. Does he begin sheep-shearing with Jack Falkner? |
| MORRIS | Nay, an you feed this vein, sir, fare you [[Well\|well]]. |
| FALKNER | Why, farewell, [[Frost\|frost]]! I’ll go hang myself out for the—poll-head. Make a Sar’cen of Jack? |
| MORRIS | Thou desperate knave, for that I see the devil<br>Wholly gets hold of thee— |
| FALKNER | The devil’s a damned rascal. |
| MORRIS | I charge thee wait on me no more; no more<br>Call me thy master. |
| FALKNER | Why then, a word, Master Morris. |
| MORRIS | I’ll hear no words, sir, fare you [[Well\|well]]. |
| FALKNER | ’Sblood, farewelll |
| MORRIS | Why dost thou follow me? |
| FALKNER | Because I’m an ass. Do you set your shavers upon me, and then cast me off? Must I condole? Have the Fates played the fools? (Weeps) Am I their cut? Now the poor sconce is taken, must Jack march with bag and baggage? |
| MORRIS | You coxcomb! |
| FALKNER | Nay, you ha’ poached me, you ha’ given me a hire, it’s here, here. |
| MORRIS | Away, you kind ass. Come, sir, dry your eyes.<br>Keep your old place, and mend these fooleries. |
| FALKNER | I care not to be turned off, an ’twere a ladder, so it be in my [[Humour\|humour]] or the Fates beckon to me. Nay, pray, sir, if the Destinies spin me a fine thread, Falkner flies another pitch. And to avoid the headache, hereafter before I’ll be a hairmonger I’ll be a whoremonger. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |
| FALKNER | [Addition IV (Dekker)]<br>[Addition V (playhouse scribe)]<br><br>Sc. 9 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Messenger to More. Messenger. T. Goodal |
| MESSENGER | My honourable lord, the Mayor of [[London]]<br>Accompanied with his lady and her train<br>Are coming hither, and are hard at hand<br>To feast with you. A sergeant’s come before<br>To tell your lordship of their near approach. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Messenger |
| MORE | Why, this is cheerful news. Friends go and come.<br>Reverend Erasmus, whose delicious words<br>Express the very [[Soul\|soul]] and life of wit,<br>Newly took sad leave of me, with [[Tears\|tears]]<br>Troubled the silver channel of the Thames,<br>Which, glad of such a burden, proudly swelled<br>And on her bosom bore him toward the sea.<br>He’s gone to Rotterdam. Peace go with him!<br>He left me heavy when he went from hence,<br>But this recomforts me. The kind [[Lord Mayor]],<br>His brethren aldermen, with their fair wives<br>Will feast this [[Night\|night]] with us. Why, so’t should be.<br>More’s merry heart lives by good company. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Master Roper and Servingmen |
| MORE | Good gentlemen, be careful; give great charge<br>Our diet be made dainty for the taste.<br>For, of all people that the earth affords,<br>The Londoners fare richest at their boards.<br><br>[Addition V (playhouse scribe)]<br>[Original Text (Munday)]<br>Come, my good fellows, stir, be diligent.<br>Sloth is an idle fellow. Leave him now.<br>The [[Time\|time]] requires your expeditious service.<br>Place me here stools to set the ladies on. |
| *(didascalia)* | Servingmen set stools |
| MORE | Son Roper, you have given order for the banquet? |
| ROPER | I have, my lord, and everything is ready. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lady More |
| MORE | O welcome, wife. Give you direction<br>How women should be placed; you know it best.<br>For my [[Lord Mayor]], his brethren, and the rest,<br>Let me [[Alone\|alone]]. Men best can order men. |
| LADY MORE | I warrant ye, my lord, all shall be [[Well\|well]].<br>There’s one without that stays to speak with ye,<br>And bade me tell ye that he is a player. |
| MORE | A player, wife?—One of ye bid him come in. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit one, [a Servingman] |
| MORE | Nay, stir there, fellows. Fie, ye are too slow!<br>See that your lights be in a readiness.<br>The banquet shall be here.—God’s me, madam,<br>Leave my Lady Mayoress? Both of us from the board?<br>And my son Roper too? What may our guests think? |
| LADY MORE | My lord, they are risen, and sitting by the [[Fire\|fire]]. |
| MORE | Why, yet go you, and keep them company.<br>It is not meet we should be absent both. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Lady |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Player |
| MORE | Welcome, good friend. What is your will with me? |
| PLAYER | My lord, my fellows and myself<br>Are come to tender ye our willing service,<br>So please you to command us. |
| MORE | What, for a play, you mean?<br>Whom do ye serve? |
| PLAYER | My Lord Cardinal’s grace. |
| MORE | My Lord Cardinal’s players? Now trust me, welcome.<br>You happen hither in a lucky [[Time\|time]]<br>To pleasure me and benefit yourselves.<br>The Mayor of [[London]] and some aldermen,<br>His lady, and their wives are my kind guests<br>This [[Night\|night]] at supper. Now, to have a play<br>Before the banquet will be excellent.<br>How think you, son Roper? |
| ROPER | ’Twill do [[Well\|well]], my lord,<br>And be right pleasing pastime to your guests. |
| MORE | I prithee tell me, what plays have ye? |
