| PLAYER | Diverse, my lord: The Cradle of Security,<br><br>Hit Nail o’th’ Head, Impatient [[Poverty]],<br><br>The Play of Four Ps, Dives and Lazarus,<br><br>Lusty Juventus, and The [[Marriage]] of Wit and Wisdom. |
| MORE | The [[Marriage]] of Wit and Wisdom? That, my lads,<br>I’ll none but that. The theme is very good,<br>And may maintain a liberal argument.<br>To marry wit to wisdom asks some cunning.<br>Many have wit that may come short of wisdom.<br>We’ll see how Master Poet plays his part,<br>And whether wit or wisdom grace his art.<br>Go, make him drink, and all his fellows too.<br>How many are ye? |
| PLAYER | Four men and a boy, sir. |
| MORE | But one boy? Then I see There’s but few women in the play. |
| PLAYER | Three, my lord: Dame Science, Lady [[Vanity]],<br>And Wisdom she herself. |
| MORE | And one boy play them all? By‘r Lady, he’s loaden.<br>[[Well]], my good fellow, get ye straight together<br>And make ye ready with what haste ye may. |
| *(didascalia)* | To Servingmen Provide their supper’gainst the play be |
| MORE | done,<br>Else shall we stay our guests here overlong. |
| *(didascalia)* | To Player Make haste, I pray ye. |
| PLAYER | We will, my lord. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Servingmen and Player |
| MORE | Where are the waits? [To Roper] Go, bid them play,<br>To spend the [[Time\|time]] a while. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lady More |
| MORE | How now, madam? |
| LADY MORE | My lord, they’re coming hither. |
| MORE | They’re welcome. Wife, I’ll tell ye one thing.<br>Our sport is somewhat mended: we shall have<br>A play tonight, The [[Marriage]] of Wit and Wisdom,<br>And acted by my good Lord Cardinal’s players.<br>How like ye that, wife? |
| LADY MORE | My Lord, I like it [[Well\|well]].<br>See, they are coming.<br>Waits play hautbois<br><br>The waits plays. Enters [[Lord Mayor]], so many Aldermen<br><br>as may, the Lady Mayoress, in scarlet, with other<br><br>ladies and [[Sir Thomas More]]’s daughters, [one of them<br><br>Roper’s wife]; Servants carrying lighted torches by them |
| MORE | Once again, welcome, welcome, my good [[Lord Mayor]],<br>And brethren all—for once I was your brother,<br>And so am still in heart. It is not state<br>That can our [[Love\|love]] from [[London]] separate.<br>〈〉<br>〈 〉 naught but [[Pride\|pride]];<br>But they that cast an eye still whence they came<br>Know how they [[Rose\|rose]], and how to use the same. |
| [[Lord Mayor\|LORD MAYOR]] | My lord, you set a gloss on [[London]]’s fame,<br>And make it happy ever by your name.<br>Needs must we say when we remember More,<br>’Twas he that drove rebellion from our door<br>With [[Grave\|grave]] discretions, mild and gentle breath,<br>Shielding a many subjects’ lives from [[Death\|death]].<br>O, how our [[City\|city]] is by you renowned,<br>And with your virtues our endeavours crowned. |
| MORE | No more, my good [[Lord Mayor]]; but thanks to all<br>That on so short a summons you would come<br>To visit him that holds your kindness dear.<br>[To Lady More] Madam, you are not merry with my Lady<br>Mayoress<br>And these fair ladies. Pray ye, seat them all.<br>[To [[Lord Mayor]]] And here, my lord, let me appoint your<br>place;<br>The rest to seat themselves. Nay, I’ll weary ye;<br>You will not long in haste to visit me. |
| LADY MORE | Good madam, sit. In sooth, you shall sit here. |
| LADY MAYORESS | Good madam, pardon me, it may not be. |
| LADY MORE | In troth, I’ll have it so. I’ll sit here by ye.<br>Good ladies, sit.—More stools here, ho! |
| LADY MAYORESS | It is your favour, madam, makes me thus<br>Presume above my merit. |
| LADY MORE | When we come to you,<br>Then shall you rule us as we rule you here.<br>[They sit]<br>Now must I tell ye, madam, we have a play<br>To welcome ye withal. How good soe’er<br>That know not I; my lord will have it so. |
| MORE | Wife, [[Hope\|hope]] the best; I am sure they’ll do their best.<br>They that would better comes not at their feast.<br>My good Lord Cardinal’s players, I thank them for it,<br>Play us a play, to lengthen out your welcome,<br>My good [[Lord Mayor]] and all my other friends.<br><br>They say it is The [[Marriage]] of Wit and Wisdom—<br>A theme of some import, howe’er it prove.<br>But if art fail, we’ll inch it out with [[Love\|love]].<br>What, are they ready? |
| SERVANT | My lord, one of the players craves to speak with you. |
| MORE | With me? Where is he? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [the Player of] Inclination the Vice, ready, [with a bridle in his hand] |
| PLAYER | of INCLINATION Here, my lord. |
| MORE | How now, what’s the matter? |
| PLAYER | of INCLINATION We would [[Desire\|desire]] your [[Honour\|honour]] but to stay a little. One of my fellows is but run to Ogle’s for a long beard for young Wit, and he’ll be here presently. |
| MORE | A long beard for young Wit? Why, man, he may be without a beard till he come to [[Marriage\|marriage]], for wit goes not all by the hair. When comes Wit in? |
| PLAYER | of INCLINATION In the second scene, next to the Prologue, my lord. |
| MORE | Why, play on till that scene come, and by that [[Time\|time]] Wit’s beard will be grown, or else the fellow returned with it. And what part play’st thou? |
| PLAYER | of INCLINATION Inclination, the Vice, my lord. |
| MORE | Gramercies, now I may take the Vice if I list. And wherefore hast thou that bridle in thy hand? |
| PLAYER | of INCLINATION I must be bridled anon, my lord. |
| MORE | An thou beest not saddled too it makes no matter, for then Wit’s Inclination may gallop so fast that he will outstrip wisdom and fall to folly. |
| PLAYER | of INCLINATION Indeed, so he does, to Lady [[Vanity]]; but we have no Folly in our play. |
| MORE | Then there’s no wit in’t, I’ll be sworn. Folly waits on wit as the [[Shadow\|shadow]] on the [[Body\|body]], and where wit is ripest, there folly still is readiest. But begin, I prithee. We’ll rather allow a beardless Wit than Wit, all beard, to have no brain. |
| PLAYER | of INCLINATION Nay, he has his apparel on too, my lord, and therefore he is the readier to enter. |
| MORE | Then, good Inclination, begin at a venture. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Player of Inclination |
| MORE | My [[Lord Mayor]], Wit lacks a beard, or else they would<br>begin.<br>I’d lend him mine, but that it is too thin.<br>[[Silence]], they come.<br>The trumpet sounds. Enter the Prologue |
| PROLOGUE | Now for as much as in these latter days<br>Throughout the whole world in every land<br>Vice doth increase and virtue decays,<br>Iniquity having the upper hand,<br>We therefore intend, good gentle audience,<br>A pretty, short interlude to play at this present,<br>Desiring your leave and quiet [[Silence\|silence]]<br>To show the same as is meet and expedient.<br>It is called The [[Marriage]] of Wit and Wisdom,<br>A matter right pithy and pleasing to hear,<br>Whereof in brief we will show the whole sum.<br>But I must be gone, for Wit doth appear. Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Wit, ruffling, and Inclination, the Vice |
| WIT | [sings]<br>In an arbour green, asleep whereas I lay—<br>The [[Birds\|birds]] sang sweetly in the midst of the day—<br>I dreamèd fast of mirth and play.<br>In youth is pleasure, in youth is pleasure.<br>Methought I walked still to and fro,<br>And from her company I could not go;<br>But when I waked it was not so. iso<br>In youth is pleasure, in youth is pleasure.<br>Therefor my heart is surely plight<br>Of her [[Alone\|alone]] to have a sight<br>Which is my joy and heart’s delight.<br>In youth is pleasure, in youth is pleasure. |
| *(didascalia)* | MORE to [[Lord Mayor]] Mark ye, my lord, this is Wit without a beard. What will he be by that [[Time\|time]] he comes to the commodity of a beard? |
| INCLINATION | [to Wit]<br>O sir, the ground is the better on which she doth go,<br>For she will make better cheer with a little she can get<br>Than many a one can with a great banquet of meat. |
| WIT | And is her name Wisdom? |
| INCLINATION | Ay, sir, a wife most fit<br>For you, my good master, my dainty sweet Wit. |
| WIT | To be in her company my heart it is set.<br>Therefore I prithee to let us be gone,<br>For unto Wisdom Wit hath inclination. |
| INCLINATION | O sir, she will come herself even anon,<br>For I told her before where we would stand,<br>And then she said she would beck us with her hand.<br>(Flourishing his dagger)<br>Back with those boys and saucy great knaves.<br>What, stand ye here so big in your braves?<br>My dagger about your coxcombs shall walk<br>If I may but so much as hear ye chat or talk. |
| WIT | But will she take pains to come for us hither? |
| INCLINATION | I warrant ye, therefore you must be familiar with her.<br>When she cometh in place<br>You must her embrace<br>Somewhat handsomely,<br>Lest she think it danger<br>Because you are a stranger<br>To come in your company. |
| WIT | I warrant thee, Inclination, I will be busy.<br>O, how Wit longs to be in Wisdom’s company! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lady [[Vanity]], singing and beckoning with her hand |
| [[Vanity\|VANITY]] | Come hither, come hither, come hither, come.<br>Such cheer as I have, thou shalt have some. |
| MORE | This is Lady [[Vanity]], I’ll hold my life.<br>Beware, good Wit, you take not her to wife. |
| INCLINATION | [to Lady [[Vanity]]]<br>What, Unknown Honesty, a word in your ear.<br>[Lady [[Vanity]]] offers to depart<br>You shall not be gone as yet, I swear.<br>Here’s none but your friends; you need not to fray.<br>This young gentleman loves ye, therefore you must stay. |
| WIT | I trust in me she will think no danger;<br>For I [[Love\|love]] [[Well\|well]] the company of fair women—<br>And, though to you I am a stranger,<br>Yet Wit may pleasure you now and then. |
| [[Vanity\|VANITY]] | Who, you? Nay, you are such a holy man<br>That to touch one you dare not be bold.<br>I think you would not kiss a young woman<br>If one would give ye twenty pound in gold. |
| WIT | Yes, in good sadness, lady, that I would.<br>I could find in my heart to kiss you in your smock. |
| [[Vanity\|VANITY]] | My back is broad enough to bear that mock;<br>For it hath been told me many a [[Time\|time]]<br>That you would be seen in no such company as mine. |
| WIT | Not Wit in the company of Lady Wisdom?<br>O Jove, for what do I hither come? |
| INCLINATION | Sir, she did this nothing else but to prove<br>Whether a little thing would you move<br>To be angry and fret.<br>What an if one said so?<br>Let such trifling matters go,<br>And with a kind kiss come out of her debt. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter another Player |
| PLAYER | of INCLINATION Is Luggins come yet with the beard? |
| OTHER PLAYER | No, [[Faith\|faith]], he is not come. Alas, what shall we do? |
| PLAYER | of INCLINATION [to More] Forsooth, we can go no further till our fellow Luggins come, for he plays Good Counsel, and now he should enter to admonish Wit that this is Lady [[Vanity]] and not Lady Wisdom. |
| MORE | Nay, an it be no more but so, ye shall not tarry at a stand for that. We’ll not have our play marred for lack of a little good counsel. Till your fellow come I’ll give him the best counsel that I can. Pardon me, my [[Lord Mayor]], I [[Love\|love]] to be merry.<br>[He rises and joins the players] |
| MORE | as GOOD COUNSEL<br>O good Master Wit, thou art now on the bow hand,<br>And blindly in thine own opinion dost stand.<br>I tell thee, this naughty lewd Inclination<br>Does lead thee amiss in a very strange fashion.<br>This is not Wisdom, but Lady [[Vanity]].<br>Therefore list to Good Counsel, and be ruled by me. |
| PLAYER | of INCLINATION In troth, my lord, it is as right to Luggins’s part as can be. Speak, Wit. |
| MORE | Nay, we will not have our audience disappointed if I can help it. |
| WIT | Art thou Good Counsel, and wilt tell me so?<br>Wouldst thou have Wit from Lady Wisdom to go?<br>Thou art some deceiver, I tell thee verily,<br>In saying that this is Lady [[Vanity]]. |
| MORE | as GOOD COUNSEL<br>Wit, judge not things by the outward show.<br>The eye oft mistakes, right [[Well\|well]] you do know.<br>Good Counsel assures thee upon his honesty<br>That this is not Wisdom, but Lady [[Vanity]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Luggins, with the beard |
| PLAYER | of INCLINATION O my lord, he is come. Now we shall go forward. |
| MORE | Art thou come? [[Well]], fellow, I have holp to save<br>thine honesty a little. Now, if thou canst give Wit any<br>better counsel than I have done, spare not. There I leave<br>him to thy [[Mercy\|mercy]].<br>But by this [[Time\|time]] I am sure our banquet’s ready.<br>My lord and ladies, we will taste that first,<br>And then they shall begin the play again,<br>Which through the fellow’s absence, and by me,<br>Instead of helping, hath been hindered.<br>[To Servants] Prepare against we come. Lights there, I<br>say.—<br>Thus fools oft-times do help to mar the play. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt. Players remain |
| PLAYER | of WIT Fie, fellow Luggins, you serve us handsomely, do ye not think ye? |
| JUGGINS | Why, Ogle was not within, and his wife would not let me have the beard, and, by my troth, I ran so fast that I sweat again. |
| PLAYER | of INCLINATION Do ye hear, fellows? Would not my lord make a rare player? O, he would uphold a company beyond all ho, better than Mason among the [[King]]’s players. Did ye mark how extemp’rically he fell to the matter, and spake Luggins’s part almost as it is in the very book set down? |
| PLAYER | of WIT Peace, do ye know what ye say? My lord a player? Let us not meddle with any such matters. Yet I may be a little proud that my lord hath answered me in my part. But come, let us go and be ready to begin the play again. |
| LUGGINS | Ay, that’s the best, for now we lack nothing.<br><br>[Original Text (Munday)]<br>[Addition VI (Heywood)] |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Servingman to the players, with a reward |
| SERVINGMAN | Where be these players? |
| ALL THE PLAYERS | Here, sir. |
| SERVINGMAN | My lord is sent for to the [[Court\|court]],<br>And all the guests do after supper part;<br>And, for he will not trouble you again,<br>By me for your reward a sends eight angels,<br>With many thanks. But sup before you go.<br>It is his will you should be fairly entreated.<br>Follow, I pray ye. |
| PLAYER | of WIT This, Luggins, is your negligence.<br>Wanting Wit’s beard brought things into dislike;<br>For otherwise the play had been all seen,<br>Where now some curious citizen disgraced it,<br>And, discommending it, all is dismissed. |
| PLAYER | of INCLINATION Fore God, a says true. But hear ye, sirs: eight angels, ha! My lord would never give’s eight angels. More or less, for twelve pence: either it should be three pounds, five pounds, or ten pounds, there twenty shillings wanting, sure. |
| PLAYER | of WIT Twenty to one, ’tis so. I have a trick. My lord comes; stand aside. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter More with attendants, with purse and mace |
| MORE | In haste, to Council? What’s the business now<br>That all so late his highness sends for me?—<br>What seek’st thou, fellow? |
| PLAYER | of WIT<br>Nay, nothing. Your lordship sent eight angels by your<br>man,<br>And I have lost two of them in the rushes. |
| MORE | Wit, look to that! Eight angels? I did send them ten.<br>Who gave it them? |
| SERVINGMAN | I, my lord. I had no more about me;<br>But by and by they shall receive the rest. |
| MORE | [[Well]], Wit, ‘twas wisely done. Thou play’st Wit [[Well\|well]]<br>indeed<br>Not to be thus deceived of thy right.<br>Am I a man by office truly ordained<br>Equally to divide true right his own,<br>And shall I have deceivers in my [[house]]?<br>Then what avails my bounty, when such servants<br>Deceive the poor of what the master gives?<br>Go one and pull his coat over his ears.<br>There are too many such. Give them their right.<br>Wit, let thy fellows thank thee; ’twas [[Well\|well]] done.<br>Thou now deservest to match with Lady Wisdom.<br>[Exeunt More and attendants] |
| PLAYER | of INCLINATION God-a-[[Mercy\|mercy]], Wit. [To the Servingman ] Sir, you had a master, [[Sir Thomas More]]. More? But now we shall have more. |
| LUGGINS | God bless him, I would there were more of his mind! A loves our quality, and yit he’s a larned man and knows what the world is.<br>[PLAYER of INCLINATION] [[Well]], a kind man, and more loving than many other, but I think we ha’ met with the first— |
| LUGGINS | First sarved his man that had our angels; and he may chance dine with Duke Humphrey tomorrow, being turned away today. Come, let’s go. |
| *(didascalia)* | [PLAYER of INCLINATION] And many such rewards would make us all ride, and [[Horse\|horse]] us with the best nags in Smithfield.Exeunt |
| LUGGINS | [Addition VI<br>(Heywood)]<br>[Original Text (Munday)]<br><br>Sc. 10 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Earls of Shrewsbury, Surrey, Bishop of Rochester, and other lords, [attended,] severally, doing courtesy to each other, Clerk of the Council waiting bare-headed |
| SURREY | Good morrow to my lord of Shrewsbury. |
| SHREWSBURY | The like unto the honoured [[Earl of Surrey]].<br>Yon comes my lord of Rochester. |
| ROCHESTER | Good morrow, my good lords. |
| SURREY | Clerk of the Council,<br>What [[Time\|time]] is’t of day? |
| CLERK | Past eight of [[Clock\|clock]], my lord. |
| SHREWSBURY | I [[Wonder\|wonder]] that my good Lord Chancellor<br>Doth stay so long, considering there’s matters<br>Of high importance to be scanned upon. |
| SURREY | Clerk of the Council, certify his lordship<br>The lords expect him here. |
| ROCHESTER | It shall not need.<br>Yon comes his lordship. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Sir Thomas More]], with purse and mace borne |
| ROCHESTER | before him |
| MORE | Good morrow to this fair assembly.<br>Come, my good lords, let’s sit.<br>They sit<br>O serious square!<br>Upon this little board is daily scanned<br>The health and preservation of the land,<br>We the physicians that effect this good,<br>Now by choice diet, anon by letting [[Blood\|blood]].<br>Our toil and careful watching brings the [[King]]<br>In league with slumbers, to which peace doth sing.—<br>Avoid the room there!—<br>What business, lords, today? |
| SHREWSBURY | This, my good lord:<br>About the entertainment of the Emperor<br>’Gainst the perfidious French into our pay. |
| SURREY | My lords, as‘tis the custom in this place<br>The youngest should speak first, so if I chance<br>In this case to speak youngly, pardon me.<br>I will agree France now hath her full strength,<br>As having new recovered the pale [[Blood\|blood]]<br>Which war sluiced forth; and I consent to this:<br>That the conjunction of our English forces<br>With arms of [[Germany]] may sooner bring<br>This prize of conquest in. But then, my lords,<br>As in the moral hunting ’twixt the [[Lion\|lion]]<br>And other beasts force joined 〈 〉<br>Frighted the weaker sharers from their parts,<br>So, if the Empire’s sovereign chance to put<br>His plea of partnership into war’s [[Court\|court]],<br>Swords should decide the difference, and our [[Blood\|blood]]<br>In private [[Tears\|tears]] lament his entertainment. |
| SHREWSBURY | To [[Doubt\|doubt]] the worst is still the wise man’s shield<br>That arms him safely, but the world knows this:<br>The Emperor is a man of royal [[Faith\|faith]].<br>His [[Love\|love]] unto our sovereign brings him down<br>From his imperial seat, to march in pay<br>Under our English [[Flag\|flag]], and wear the [[Cross\|cross]]<br>Like some high order on his manly breast.<br>Thus serving, he’s not master of himself,<br>But, like a colonel, commanding other,<br>Is by the general overawed himself. |
| ROCHESTER | Yet, my good lord— |
| SHREWSBURY | Let me conclude my speech.<br>As subjects share no portion in the conquest<br>Of their true sovereign other than the merit<br>That from the sovereign guerdons the true subject,<br>So the good Emperor in a friendly league<br>Of amity with England will not soil<br>His [[Honour\|honour]] with the theft of English spoil. |
| MORE | There is no question but this entertainment<br>Will be most honourable, most commodious.<br>I have oft heard good captains wish to have<br>Rich soldiers to attend them, such as would fight<br>Both for their lives and livings. Such a one<br>Is the good Emperor. I would to God<br>We had ten thousand of such able men.<br>Ha, then there would appear no [[Court\|court]], no [[City\|city]],<br>But, where the wars were, they would pay<br>themselves.<br>Then, to prevent in French wars England’s loss,<br>Let German flags wave with our English [[Cross\|cross]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir Thomas Palmer |
| PALMER | My lords, his majesty hath sent by me<br>These articles enclosed, first to be viewed,<br>And then to be subscribed to. (With great reverence) I<br>tender them<br>In that due reverence which befits this place. |
| MORE | Subscribe these articles? Stay, let us pause.<br>Our [[Conscience\|conscience]] first shall parley with our laws.<br>My lord of Rochester, view you the paper. |
| ROCHESTER | Subscribe to these? Now good Sir Thomas Palmer,<br>Beseech the [[King]] that he will pardon me.<br>My heart will check my hand whilst I do write.<br>Subscribing so, I were an hypocrite. |
| PALMER | Do you refuse it then, my lord? |
| ROCHESTER | I do, Sir Thomas. |
| PALMER | Then here I summon you forthwith t’appear<br><br>Before his majesty, to answer there<br><br>This capital contempt. |
| ROCHESTER | I rise, and part,<br>In lieu of this, to tender him my heart. He riseth |
| PALMER | Will’t please your [[Honour\|honour]] to subscribe, my lord? |
| MORE | Sir, tell his highness, I entreat<br><br>Some [[Time\|time]] for to bethink me of this task.<br><br>In the meanwhile, I do resign mine office<br><br>Into my sovereign’s hands. All alter |
| PALMER | Then, my lord,<br>Hear the prepared order from the [[King]].<br><br>On your refusal you shall straight depart<br><br>Unto your [[house]] at Chelsea, till you know<br><br>Our sovereign’s further pleasure. |
| MORE | Most willingly I go.<br>My lords, if you will visit me at Chelsea<br><br>We’ll go a-fishing, and with a cunning net,<br><br>Not like weak film, we’ll catch none but the great.<br><br>Farewell, my noble lords. Why, this is right:<br><br>Good morrow to the sun, to state good [[Night\|night]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| PALMER | Will you subscribe, my lords? |
| SURREY | Instantly, good Sir Thomas.<br>They write<br>We’ll bring the writing unto our sovereign. |
| PALMER | My lord of Rochester,<br>You must with me, to answer this contempt. |
| ROCHESTER | This is the worst.<br>Who’s freed from life is from all care exempt. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Rochester and Palmer |
| SURREY | Now let us hasten to our sovereign.<br><br>’Tis strange that my Lord Chancellor should refuse<br>The duty that the law of God bequeaths<br>Unto the [[King\|king]]. |
| SHREWSBURY | Come, let us in. No [[Doubt\|doubt]]<br>His mind will alter, and the Bishop’s too.<br>Error in learnèd heads hath much to do.<br>[Exeunt]<br><br>Sc. 11 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Lady More, her two Daughters, [one of them Roper’s Wife,] and Master Roper, as walking |
| ROPER | Madam, what ails ye for to look so sad? |
| LADY MORE | Troth, son, I know not what. I am not sick,<br>And yet I am not [[Well\|well]]. I would be merry,<br>But somewhat lies so heavy on my heart<br>I cannot choose but sigh. You are a scholar.<br>I pray ye tell me, may one credit [[Dreams\|dreams]]? |
| ROPER | Why ask you that, dear madam? |
| LADY MORE | Because tonight I had the strangest dream<br>That e‘er my [[Sleep\|sleep]] was troubled with.<br>Methought ’twas [[Night\|night]],<br>And that the [[King]] and [[Queen]] went on the Thames<br>In barges to hear music. My lord and I<br>Were in a little boat, methought—Lord, Lord,<br>What strange things live in slumbers!—and being near,<br>We grappled to the barge that bare the [[King]];<br>But after many pleasing voices spent<br>In that still-moving music [[house]], methought<br>The violence of the stream did sever us<br>Quite from the golden fleet and hurried us<br>Unto the Bridge which, with unused horror,<br>We entered at full tide; thence some flight shoot<br>Being carried by the waves, our boat stood still<br>Just opposite the Tower; and there it turned<br>And turned about, as when a whirlpool sucks<br>The circled waters. Methought that we both cried,<br>Till that we sunk, where arm in arm we died. |
| ROPER | Give no respect, dear madam, to fond [[Dreams\|dreams]].<br>They are but slight illusions of the [[Blood\|blood]]. |
| LADY MORE | Tell me not all are so, for often [[Dreams\|dreams]]<br>Are true diviners, either of good or ill.<br>I cannot be in quiet till I hear<br>How my lord fares. |
| ROPER | (aside)<br>Nor I.—Come hither, wife.<br>I will not fright thy mother to interpret<br>The [[Nature\|nature]] of [[A Dream\|a dream]]; but, trust me, sweet,<br>This [[Night\|night]] I have been troubled with thy father<br>Beyond all thought. |
| ROPER’S WIFE | [aside to Roper] Truly, and so have I.<br>Methought I saw him here in Chelsea church,<br>Standing upon the rood-loft, now defaced;<br>And whilst he kneeled and prayed before the image<br>It fell with him into the upper choir,<br>Where my poor father lay all stained in [[Blood\|blood]]. |
| ROPER | [aside to his Wife]<br>Our [[Dreams\|dreams]] all meet in one conclusion,<br>Fatal, I [[Fear\|fear]]. |
| LADY MORE | What’s that you talk? I pray ye let me know it. |
| ROPER’S WIFE | Nothing, good mother. |
| LADY MORE | This is your fashion still: I must know nothing.<br>Call Master Catesby; he shall straight to [[Court\|court]]<br>And see how my lord does. I shall not rest<br>Until my heart lean panting on his breast. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Sir Thomas More]], merrily, servants attending |
| MORE’S OTHER DAUGHTER | See where my father comes, joyful and merry. |
| MORE | As seamen, having passed a troubled [[Storm\|storm]],<br>[[Dance]] on the pleasant shore, so I—O, I could speak<br>Now like a poet! Now, afore God, I am passing light.<br>Wife, give me kind welcome.<br>[He kisses her]<br>Thou wast wont to blame<br>My kissing when my beard was in the stubble;<br>But I have been trimmed of late: I have had<br>A smooth [[Court\|court]] shaving, in good [[Faith\|faith]], I have.<br>Daughters<br>kneel<br>[To Daughters] God bless ye.—Son Roper, give me your<br>hand. |
| ROPER | Your [[Honour\|honour]]’s welcome [[Home\|home]]. |
| MORE | [[Honour]]? Ha, ha!<br>And how dost, wife? |
| ROPER | [aside] He bears himself most strangely. |
| LADY MORE | Will your lordship in? |
| MORE | Lordship? No, wife, that’s gone.<br>The ground was slight that we did lean upon. |
| LADY MORE | Lord, that your [[Honour\|honour]] ne’er will leave these jests!<br>In [[Faith\|faith]], it ill becomes ye. |
| MORE | O good wife,<br>[[Honour]] and jests are both together fled.<br>The merriest councillor of England’s dead. |
| LADY MORE | Who’s that, my lord? |
| MORE | Still ‘lord’? The Lord Chancellor, wife. |
| LADY MORE | That’s you. |
| MORE | Certain, but I have changed my life.<br>Am I not leaner than I was before?<br>The fat is gone. My title’s only ‘More’.<br>Contented with one style, I’ll live at rest.<br>They that have many names are not still best.<br>I have resigned mine office. Count’st me not wise? |
| LADY MORE | O God! |
| MORE | Come, breed not female children in your eyes.<br>The [[King]] will have it so. |
| LADY MORE | What’s the offence? |
| MORE | Tush, let that pass; we’ll talk of that anon.<br>The [[King]] seems a physician to my fate.<br>His princely mind would train me back to state. |
| ROPER | Then be his patient, my most honoured father. |
| MORE | O son Roper,<br>Ubi turpis est medicina, sanari piget.<br><br>No, wife, be merry, and be merry all.<br>You smiled at rising; weep not at my fall.<br>Let’s in, and here joy like to private friends,<br>Since days of pleasure have repentant ends.<br>The light of [[Greatness\|greatness]] is with triumph borne;<br>It sets at midday oft, with public scorn. Exeunt<br><br>Sc. 12 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Bishop of Rochester, Surrey, Shrewsbury, Lieutenant of the Tower, and warders with weapons |
| ROCHESTER | Your kind persuasions, honourable lords,<br>I can but thank ye for, but in this breast<br>There lives a [[Soul\|soul]] that aims at higher things<br>Than temporary pleasing earthly kings.<br>God bless his highness, even with all my heart.<br>We shall meet one day, though that now we part. |
| SURREY | We not misdoubt your wisdom can discern<br>What best befits it; yet in [[Love\|love]] and zeal<br>We could entreat it might be otherwise. |
| SHREWSBURY | [to Rochester]<br>No [[Doubt\|doubt]] your fatherhood will by yourself<br>Consider better of the present case,<br>And grow as great in favour as before. |
| ROCHESTER | For that, as pleaseth God, in my restraint<br>From worldly causes I shall better see<br>Into myself than at proud liberty.<br>The Tower and I will privately confer<br>Of things wherein at [[Freedom\|freedom]] I may err.<br>But I am troublesome unto your honours,<br>And hold ye longer than becomes my duty.<br>Master Lieutenant, I am now your charge;<br>And, though you keep my [[Body\|body]], yet my [[Love\|love]]<br>Waits on my [[King\|king]] and you while Fisher lives. |
| SURREY | Farewell, my lord of Rochester. We’ll pray<br>or your release, and labour’t as we may. |
| SHREWSBURY | [to Rochester]<br>Thereof assure yourself. So do we leave ye,<br>And to your happy private thoughts bequeath ye. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Lords |
| ROCHESTER | Now, Master Lieutenant, on; i’ God’s name, go;<br>And with as glad a mind go I with you<br>As ever truant bade the school adieu. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |
| ROCHESTER | Sc. 13 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Sir Thomas More]], his Lady, Daughters, one of them Roper’s Wife, Master Roper, Gentlemen and |
| *(didascalia)* | Servants amongst them Catesby and Gough as in his [[house]] |
| ROCHESTER | at Chelsea. Low stools |
| MORE | Good morrow, good son Roper. [To Lady More] Sit, good<br>madam,<br>Upon an humble seat; the [[Time\|time]] so craves.<br>Rest your good heart on earth, the roof of graves.<br>You see the floor of [[Greatness\|greatness]] is uneven,<br>The cricket and high throne alike near heaven.<br>Now, daughters, you that like to branches spread<br>And give best [[Shadow\|shadow]] to a private [[house]]:<br>Be comforted, my girls. Your hopes stand fair.<br>Virtue breeds gentry; she makes the best heir. |
| BOTH DAUGHTERS | Good morrow to your [[Honour\|honour]]. |
| MORE | Nay, good [[Night\|night]] rather.<br>Your [[Honour\|honour]]’s crest-fall’n with your happy father. |
| ROPER | O, what formality, what square observance,<br>Lives in a little room! Here public care<br>Gags not the eyes of slumber. Here fierce riot<br>Ruffles not proudly in a coat of trust<br>Whilst, like a pawn at chess, he keeps in rank<br>With kings and mighty fellows. Yet indeed,<br>Those men that stand on tiptoe smile to see<br>Him pawn his fortunes. |
| MORE | True, son, here’s not so,<br>Nor does the wanton tongue here screw itself<br>Into the ear, that like a vice drinks up<br>The iron instrument. |
| LADY MORE | We are here at peace. |
| MORE | Then peace, good wife. |
| LADY MORE | For keeping still in compass—a strange point<br>In [[Time\|time]]’s new navigation—we have sailed<br>Beyond our course. |
| MORE | Have done. |
| LADY MORE | We are exiled the [[Court\|court]]. |
| MORE | Still thou harp’st on that.<br>‘Tis sin for to deserve that banishment;<br>But he that ne’er knew [[Court\|court]] courts sweet content. |
| LADY MORE | O, but dear husband— |
| MORE | I will not hear thee, wife.<br>The winding labyrinth of thy strange discourse<br>Will ne’er have end. Sit still, and, my good wife,<br>Entreat thy tongue be stilt—or, credit me,<br>Thou shalt not understand a word we speak.<br>We’ll talk in Latin.<br>[To Roper] Humida vallis raros patitur fulminis ictus.<br>More rest enjoys the subject meanly bred<br>Than he that bears the kingdom in his head. |
| ROPER | Great men are still musicians, else the world lies:<br><br>They learn low strains after the notes that rise.<br><br>Good sir, be still yourself, and but remember<br>How in this general [[Court\|court]] of short-lived pleasure<br>The world, creation is the ample food<br>That is digested in the maw of [[Time\|time]].<br>If man himself be subject to such ruin,<br>How shall his garment then, or the loose points<br>That tie respect unto his awe-ful place,<br>Avoid destruction? Most honoured father-in-law,<br>The [[Blood\|blood]] you have bequeathed these several hearts<br>To nourish your posterity stands firm;<br>And as with joy you led us first to rise,<br>So with like hearts we’ll lock preferment’s eyes.<br>[Original Text (Munday)]<br>[Addition I (Chettle)] |
| MORE | Now will I speak like More in [[Melancholy\|melancholy]];<br>For if griefs power could with her sharpest darts<br>Pierce my firm bosom, here’s sufficient cause<br>To take my farewell of mirth’s hurtless laws.<br>Poor humbled lady, thou that wert of late<br><br>Placed with the noblest women of the land,<br><br>Invited to their angel companies,<br><br>Seeming a bright star in the courtly sphere:<br><br>Why shouldst thou like a widow sit thus low,<br><br>And all thy fair consorts move from the clouds<br><br>That overdrip thy [[Beauty\|beauty]] and thy worth?<br><br>I’ll tell thee the true cause. The [[Court\|court]], like heaven,<br><br>Examines not the anger of the prince,<br><br>And, being more frail-composed of gilded earth,<br><br>Shines upon them on whom the [[King\|king]] doth shine,<br><br>Smiles if he smile, declines if he decline,<br><br>Yet, seeing both are mortal, [[Court\|court]] and [[King\|king]]<br><br>Shed not one tear for any earthly thing.<br><br>For, so God pardon me, in my saddest hour<br>Thou hast no more occasion to lament,<br>Nor these, nor those, my [[Exile\|exile]] from the [[Court\|court]]-<br>No, nor this [[Body\|body]]’s torture, were’t imposed,<br>As commonly disgraces of great men<br>Are the forewarnings of a hasty [[Death\|death]]—<br>Than to behold me after many a toil<br>Honoured with endless rest. Perchance the [[King]],<br>Seeing the [[Court\|court]] is full of [[Vanity\|vanity]],<br>Has pity lest our souls should be misled<br>And sends us to a life contemplative.<br>O, happy banishment from worldly [[Pride\|pride]],<br>When souls by private life are sanctified! |
| WIFE | O, but I [[Fear\|fear]] some plot against your life. |
| MORE | Why then, ‘tis thus: the [[King]], of his high grace,<br>Seeing my faithful service to his state,<br>Intends to send me to the [[King]] of Heaven<br>For a rich present; where my [[Soul\|soul]] shall prove<br>A true rememb’rer of his majesty.<br>Come, prithee mourn not. The worst chance is [[Death\|death]],<br>And that brings endless joy for fickle breath. |
| WIFE | Ah, but your children. |
| MORE | Tush, let them [[Alone\|alone]].<br>Say they be stripped from this poor painted cloth,<br>This outside of the earth, left houseless, bare;<br>They have minds instructed how to gather more.<br>There’s no man that’s ingenious can be poor.<br>And therefore do not weep, my little ones,<br>Though you lose all the earth. Keep your souls even<br>And you shall find inheritance in heaven.<br>But for my servants: there’s my chiefest care.<br>[To Catesby] Come hither, faithful steward. Be not<br>grieved<br>That in thy person I discharge both thee<br>And all thy other fellow officers;<br>For my great master hath discharged me.<br>If thou by serving me hast suffered loss,<br><br>Then benefit thyself by leaving me.<br><br>I [[Hope\|hope]] thou hast not; for such times as these<br><br>Bring gain to officers, whoever leese.<br><br>Great lords have only name; but in the fall<br><br>Lord Spend-All’s steward’s Master Gather-All.<br><br>But I suspect not thee. Admit thou hast.<br><br>It’s good the servants save when masters waste.<br><br>But you, poor gentlemen, that had no place<br>T’enrich yourselves but by loathed bribery,<br>Which I abhorred, and never found you loved:<br>Think, when an oak falls, underwood shrinks down,<br>And yet may live, though bruised. I pray ye strive<br>To shun my ruin; for the axe is set<br>Even at my root, to fell me to the ground.<br>The best I can do to prefer you all<br>With my mean store expect; for heaven can tell<br>That More loves all his followers more than [[Well\|well]].<br>[Addition I (Chettle)]<br>[Original Text (Munday)] |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Servant |
| SERVANT | My lord, there are new lighted at the gate<br>The Earls of Surrey and of Shrewsbury,<br>And they expect you in the inner [[Court\|court]]. |
| MORE | Entreat their lordships come into the hall. |
| LADY MORE | O God, what news with them? |
| MORE | Why, how now, wife?<br>They are but come to visit their old friend. |
| LADY MORE | O God, I [[Fear\|fear]], I [[Fear\|fear]]. |
| MORE | What shouldst thou [[Fear\|fear]], fond woman?<br>Iustum, si fractus illabatur orbis, impavidum ferient ruinae.<br><br>Here let me live estranged from great men’s looks.<br>They are like golden flies on leaden hooks. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Earls for Surrey and Shrewsbury], Downes, with his mace, and Attendants |
| SHREWSBURY | Good morrow, good Sir Thomas. |
| SURREY | [to Lady More]<br>Good day, good madam.<br>Kind salutations |
| MORE | Welcome, my good lords.<br>What ails your lordships look so [[Melancholy\|melancholy]]?<br>O, I know: you live in [[Court\|court]], and the [[Court\|court]] diet<br>Is only friend to physic. |
| SURREY | O Sir Thomas,<br>Our words are now the [[King]]‘s, and our sad looks<br>The interest of your [[Love\|love]]. We are sent to you<br>From our mild sovereign once more to demand<br>If you’ll subscribe unto those articles<br>He sent ye th’other day. Be [[Well\|well]] advised,<br>For, on my [[Honour\|honour]], lord, [[Grave\|grave]] Doctor Fisher,<br>Bishop of Rochester, at the [[Self\|self]]-same instant<br>Attached with you, is sent unto the Tower<br>For the like obstinacy. His majesty<br>Hath only sent you prisoner to your [[house]],<br>But, if you now refuse for to subscribe,<br>A stricter course will follow. |
| LADY MORE | (kneeling and weeping)<br>O dear husband— |
| BOTH DAUGHTERS | (kneeling and weeping) Dear father— |
| MORE | See, my lords,<br>This partner and these subjects to my flesh<br>Prove rebels to my [[Conscience\|conscience]]. But, my good lords,<br>If I refuse, must I unto the Tower? |
| SHREWSBURY | You must, my lord. [Gesturing to Downes] Here is an officer<br>Ready for to arrest you of high treason. |
| LADY MORE | and DAUGHTERS<br>O God, O God! |
| ROPER | Be patient, good madam. |
| MORE | Ay, Downes, is’t thou? I once did save thy life,<br>When else by cruel riotous assault<br>Thou hadst been torn in pieces. Thou art reserved<br>To be my summ‘ner to yon spiritual [[Court\|court]].<br>Give me thy hand, good fellow. Smooth thy face.<br>The diet that thou drink’st is spiced with mace,<br>And I could ne‘er abide it. ’Twill not digest,<br>’Twill lie too heavy, man, on my weak breast. |
| SHREWSBURY | Be brief, my lord, for we are limited<br>Unto an hour. |
| MORE | Unto an hour? ’Tis [[Well\|well]].<br>The bell, earth’s thunder, soon shall toll my knell. |
| LADY MORE | (kneeling)<br>Dear loving husband, if you respect not me,<br>Yet think upon your daughters. |
| MORE | (pondering to himself) Wife, stand up.<br>I have bethought me;<br>And I’ll now satisfy the [[King]]’s good pleasure. |
| BOTH DAUGHTERS | O happy alteration! |
| SHREWSBURY | Come then, subscribe, my lord. |
| SURREY | I am right glad of this your fair [[Conversion\|conversion]]. |
| MORE | O pardon me,<br>I will subscribe to go unto the Tower<br>With all submissive willingness, and thereto add<br>My bones to strengthen the foundation<br>Of [[Julius Caesar]]’s palace. Now, my lord,<br>I’ll satisfy the [[King]] even with my [[Blood\|blood]].<br>Nor will I wrong your patience. [To Downes] Friend, do<br>thine office. |
| DOWNES | [[Sir Thomas More]], Lord Chancellor of England, I arrest you in the [[King]]’s name of high treason. |
| MORE | Gramercies, friend.<br>To a great [[Prison\|prison]], to discharge the strife<br>Commenced ‘twixt [[Conscience\|conscience]] and my frailer life, 185<br>More now must march. Chelsea, adieu, adieu.<br>Strange farewell: thou shalt ne’er more see More true,<br>For I shall ne‘er see thee more.—Servants, farewell.—<br>Wife, mar not thine indifferent face. Be wise.<br>More’s widow’s husband, he must make thee rise.—<br>Daughters, ( ) what’s here, what’s here?<br>Mine eye had almost parted with a tear.—<br>Dear son, possess my virtue; that I ne’er gave.<br>[[Grave]] More thus lightly walks to a quick [[Grave\|grave]]. |
| ROPER | Curae leves loquuntur, ingentes stupent. |
| MORE | You that way in. Mind you my course in [[Prayer\|prayer]].<br>By [[Water\|water]] I to [[Prison\|prison]], to heaven through air. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [More, Downes, and Attendants at one door, the rest at another] |
| MORE | Sc. 14 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Warders of the Tower, with halberds |
| FIRST WARDER | Ho, make a guard there! |
| SECOND WARDER | Master Lieutenant gives a strait command<br>The people be avoided from the bridge. |
| THIRD WARDER | From whence is he committed, who can tell? |
| FIRST WARDER | From Durham [[house\|House]], I hear. |
| SECOND WARDER | The guard were waiting there an hour ago. |
| THIRD WARDER | If he stay long, he’ll not get near the wharf,<br>There’s such a crowd of boats upon the Thames. |
| FIRST WARDER | [[Well]], be it spoken without offence to any,<br>A wiser or more virtuous gentleman<br>Was never bred in England. |
| SECOND WARDER | I think the poor will bury him in [[Tears\|tears]].<br>I never heard a man since I was born<br>So generally bewailed of everyone. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a poor Woman [with others in a crowd] |
| THIRD WARDER | What means this woman?—Whither dost thou press? |
| FIRST WARDER | This woman will be trod to [[Death\|death]] anon. |
| SECOND WARDER | [to the Woman] What makest thou here? |
| WOMAN | To speak with that good man [[Sir Thomas More]]. |
| FIRST WARDER | To speak with him? He’s not Lord Chancellor. |
| WOMAN | The more’s the pity, sir, if it pleased God. |
| FIRST WARDER | Therefore if thou hast a petition to deliver<br>Thou mayst keep it now, for anything I know. |
| WOMAN | I am a poor woman, and have had, God knows,<br>A suit this two year in the Chancery,<br>And he hath all the evidence I have,<br>Which should I lose I am utterly undone. |
| FIRST WARDER | [[Faith]], and I [[Fear\|fear]] thou‘It hardly come by ’em now.<br>I am sorry for thee even with all my heart. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Lords [of Shrewsbury and Surrey], with [[Sir Thomas More]], and attendants; and enter Lieutenant and Gentleman Porter |
| SECOND WARDER | Woman, stand back. You must avoid this place.<br>The lords must pass this way into the Tower. |
| MORE | I thank your lordships for your pains thus far<br>To my strong-[[house]]. |
| WOMAN | Now good [[Sir Thomas More]], for [[Christ]]’s dear sake<br>Deliver me my writings back again<br>That do concern my title. |
| MORE | What, my old client, art thou got hither too?<br>Poor silly wretch, I must confess indeed<br>I had such writings as concern thee near,<br>But the [[King]]<br>Has ta’en the matter into his own hand;<br>He has all I had. Then, woman, sue to him.<br>I cannot help thee. Thou must bear with me. |
| WOMAN | Ah, gentle heart, my [[Soul\|soul]] for thee is sad.<br>Farewell, the best friend that the poor e’er had. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| GENTLEMAN PORTER | Before you enter through the Tower gate,<br>Your upper garment, sir, belongs to me. |
| MORE | Sir, you shall have it. There it is.<br>He gives him his cap |
| GENTLEMAN PORTER | The upmost on your back, sir. You mistake me. |
| MORE | Sir, now I understand ye very [[Well\|well]].<br>But that you name my back,<br>Sure else my cap had been the uppermost. |
| SHREWSBURY | Farewell, kind lord. God send us merry meeting. |
| MORE | Amen, my lord. |
| SURREY | Farewell, dear friend. I [[Hope\|hope]] your safe return. |
| MORE | My lord, and my dear fellow in the Muses,<br>Farewell. Farewell, most noble poet. |
| LIEUTENANT | Adieu, most honoured lords. Exeunt Lords |
| MORE | Fair [[Prison\|prison]], welcome. Yet methinks<br>For thy fair building ‘tis too foul a name.<br>Many a guilty [[Soul\|soul]], and many an [[Innocent\|innocent]],<br>Have breathed their farewell to thy hollow rooms.<br>I oft have entered into thee this way,<br>Yet, I thank God, ne’er with a clearer [[Conscience\|conscience]]<br>Than at this hour.<br>This is my comfort yet: how hard soe’er<br>My lodging prove, the cry of the poor suitor,<br>Fatherless [[Orphan\|orphan]], or distressèd widow<br>Shall not disturb me in my quiet [[Sleep\|sleep]].<br>On then, i’ God’s name, to our close abode.<br>God is as strong here as he is abroad. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |
| MORE | Sc. 15 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Butler, Brewer, Porter, and [[Horse]]-keeper, several ways |
| BUTLER | Robin Brewer, how now, man? What cheer, what cheer? |
| BREWER | [[Faith]], Ned Butler, sick of thy disease, and these our other fellows here, Ralph [[Horse]]-keeper and Giles Porter: sad, sad. They say my lord goes to his trial today. |
| [[Horse\|HORSE]]-KEEPER | To it, man? Why, he is now at it. God send him [[Well\|well]] to [[Speed\|speed]]! |
| PORTER | Amen. Even as I wish to mine own [[Soul\|soul]], so [[Speed\|speed]] it with my honourable lord and master [[Sir Thomas More]]! |
| BUTLER | I cannot tell—I have nothing to do with matters above my capacity—but, as God judge me, if I might speak my mind, I think there lives not a more harmless gentleman in the universal world. |
| BREWER | Nor a wiser, nor a merrier, nor an honester. Go to, I’ll put that in upon mine own knowledge. |
| PORTER | Nay, an ye bate him his due of his housekeeping, hang ye all! Ye have many lord chancellors comes in debt at the year’s end, and for very housekeeping! |
| [[Horse\|HORSE]]-KEEPER | [[Well]], he was too good a lord for us, and therefore, I [[Fear\|fear]], God himself will take him. But I’ll be hanged if ever I have such another service. |
| BREWER | Soft, man, we are not discharged yet. My lord may come [[Home\|home]] again, and all will be [[Well\|well]]. |
| BUTLER | I much mistrust it. When they go to ’raigning once, there’s ever foul weather for a great while after. Enter Gough and Catesby, with a paper<br>But soft, here comes Master Gough and Master Catesby.<br>Now we shall hear more. |
| [[Horse\|HORSE]]-KEEPER | Before God, they are very sad. I [[Doubt\|doubt]] my lord is condemned. |
| PORTER | God bless his [[Soul\|soul]], and a fig then for all worldly condemnation! |
| GOUGH | [[Well]] said, Giles Porter, I commend thee for it.<br>’Twas spoken like a [[Well\|well]]-affected servant<br>Of him that was a kind lord to us all. |
| CATESBY | Which now no more he shall be, for, dear fellows,<br>Now we are masterless. Though he may live<br>So long as please the [[King]], but law hath made him<br>A dead man to the world, and given the axe his head,<br>But his sweet [[Soul\|soul]] to live among the saints. |
| GOUGH | Let us entreat ye to go call together<br>The rest of your sad fellows—by the roll<br>You’re just seven score—and tell them what ye hear<br>A virtuous, honourable lord hath done<br>Even for the meanest follower that he had.<br>This writing found my lady in his study<br>This instant morning, wherein is set down<br>Each servant’s name, according to his place<br>And office in the [[house]]. On every man<br>He frankly hath bestown twenty nobles,<br>The best and worst together, all alike,<br>Which Master Catesby hereforth will pay ye. |
| CATESBY | Take it as it is meant, a kind remembrance<br>Of a far kinder lord, with whose sad fall<br>He gives up [[house]], and farewell to us all.<br>Thus the fair spreading oak falls not [[Alone\|alone]],<br>But all the neighbour plants and under-trees<br>Are crushed down with his weight. No more of this.<br>Come and receive your due, and after go<br>Fellow-like hence, co-partners of one woe. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |
| CATESBY | Sc. 16 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Sir Thomas More]], the Lieutenant, and a Servant attending, as in his chamber in the Tower |
| MORE | Master Lieutenant, is the warrant come?<br>If it be so, i’ God’s name let us know it. |
| LIEUTENANT | My lord, it is. |
| MORE | ’Tis welcome, sir, to me with all my heart.<br>His blessèd will be done. |
| LIEUTENANT | Your wisdom, sir, hath been so [[Well\|well]] approved,<br>And your fair patience in [[Imprisonment\|imprisonment]]<br>Hath ever shown such constancy of mind<br>And Christian resolution in all troubles,<br>As warrants us you are not unprepared. |
| MORE | No, Master Lieutenant. I thank my God<br>I have peace of [[Conscience\|conscience]], though the world and I<br>Are at a little odds. But we’ll be even now, I [[Hope\|hope]],<br>Ere long. When is the execution of your warrant? |
| LIEUTENANT | Tomorrow morning. |
| MORE | So, sir, I thank ye.<br>I have not lived so ill I [[Fear\|fear]] to die.<br>Master Lieutenant,<br>I have had a sore fit of the stone tonight;<br>But the [[King]] hath sent me such a rare receipt,<br>I thank him, as I shall not need to [[Fear\|fear]] it much. |
| LIEUTENANT | In life and [[Death\|death]], still merry [[Sir Thomas More]].<br>[To Servant] Sirrah fellow, reach me the urinal.<br>He gives it him<br>Ha, let me see. There’s gravel in the [[Water\|water]].<br>And yet, in very sober [[Truth\|truth]] I swear,<br>The man were likely to live long enough,<br>So pleased the [[King]]. Here, fellow, take it. |
| SERVANT | Shall I go with it to the doctor, sir? |
| MORE | No, save thy labour. We’ll cozen him of a fee.<br>Thou shalt see me take a dram tomorrow morning<br>Shall cure the stone, I warrant, [[Doubt\|doubt]] it not.—<br>Master Lieutenant, what news of my lord of Rochester? |
| LIEUTENANT | Yesterday morning was he put to [[Death\|death]]. |
| MORE | The peace of [[Soul\|soul]] [[Sleep\|sleep]] with him!<br>He was a learned and a reverend prelate,<br>And a rich man, believe me. |
| LIEUTENANT | If he were rich, what is [[Sir Thomas More]],<br>That all this while hath been Lord Chancellor? |
| MORE | Say ye so, Master Lieutenant? What do you think<br>A man that with my [[Time\|time]] had held my place<br>Might purchase? |
| LIEUTENANT | Perhaps, my lord, two thousand pound a year. |
| MORE | Master Lieutenant, I protest to you,<br>I never had the means in all my life<br>To purchase one poor hundred pound a year.<br>I think I am the poorest chancellor<br>That ever was in England, though I could wish,<br>For credit of the place, that my estate were better. |
| LIEUTENANT | It’s very strange. |
| MORE | It will be found as true.<br>I think, sir, that with most part of my coin<br>I have purchased as strange commodities<br>As ever you heard tell of in your life. |
| LIEUTENANT | Commodities, my lord?<br>Might I without offence enquire of them? |
| MORE | Crutches, Master Lieutenant, and bare cloaks,<br>For halting soldiers and poor needy scholars,<br>Have had my gettings in the Chancery.<br>To think but what acheat the [[Crown\|crown]] shall have<br>By my attainder! I prithee, if thou beest a gentleman,<br>Get but a copy of my inventory.<br>That part of poet that was given me<br>Made me a very unthrift;<br>For this is the disease attends us all:<br>Poets were never thrifty, never shall. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lady More, mourning, Daughters, [one of them Roper’s Wife,] Master Roper |
| LIEUTENANT | O noble More!<br>My lord, your wife, your son-in-law and daughters. |
| MORE | Son Roper, welcome. Welcome, wife and girls.<br>Why do you weep? Because I live at ease?<br>Did you not see, when I was Chancellor<br>I was so cloyed with suitors every hour<br>I could not [[Sleep\|sleep]] nor dine nor sup in quiet.<br>Here’s none of this. Here I can sit and talk<br>With my honest keeper half a day together,<br>Laugh and be merry. Why then should you weep? |
| ROPER | These [[Tears\|tears]], my lord, for this your long restraint<br>[[Hope]] had dried up, with comfort that we yet,<br>Although imprisoned, might have had your life. |
| MORE | To live in [[Prison\|prison]]: what a life were that?<br>The [[King]], I thank him, loves me more than so.<br>Tomorrow I shall be at liberty<br>To go even whither I can,<br>After I have dispatched my business. |
| LADY MORE | Ah husband, husband, yet submit yourself.<br>Have care of your poor wife and children. |
| MORE | Wife, so I have, and I do leave you all<br>To His protection hath the power to keep<br>You safer than I can,<br>The father of the widow and the [[Orphan\|orphan]]. |
| ROPER | The world, my lord, hath ever held you wise,<br>And ’t shall be no distaste unto your wisdom<br>To yield to the opinion of the state. |
| MORE | I have deceived myself, I must acknowledge;<br>And as you say, son Roper, to confess the same<br>It will be no disparagement at all. |
| LADY MORE | (offering to depart)<br>His highness shall be certified thereof, immediately. |
| MORE | Nay, hear me, wife. First let me tell ye how<br>I thought to have had a barber for my beard;<br>Now I remember that were labour lost:<br>The headsman now shall cut off head and all. |
| ROPER’S WIFE | Father, his majesty, upon your meek submission,<br>Will yet, they say, receive you to his grace,<br>In as great credit as you were before. |
| MORE | ( ) wench. [[Faith]], my lord the [[King]]<br>Has appointed me to do a little business.<br>If that were past, my girl, thou then shouldst see<br>What I would say to him about that matter.<br>But I shall be so busy until then<br>I shall not tend it. |
| BOTH DAUGHTERS | Ah, my dear father! |
| LADY MORE | Dear lord and husband! |
| MORE | Be comforted, good wife, to live and [[Love\|love]] my children,<br>For with thee leave I all my care of them.<br>Son Roper, for my sake that have loved thee [[Well\|well]],<br>And for her virtue’s sake, cherish my [[Child\|child]].—<br>Girl, be not proud, but of thy husband’s [[Love\|love]].<br>Ever retain thy virtuous modesty.<br>That modesty is such a comely garment<br>As it is never out of fashion, sits as fair<br>Upon the meaner woman as the empress.<br>No stuff that gold can buy is half so rich,<br>Nor ornament that so becomes a woman.<br>Live all, and [[Love\|love]] together, and thereby<br>You give your father a rich obsequy. |
| BOTH DAUGHTERS | Your blessing, dear father. |
| MORE | I must be gone—God bless you—<br>To talk with God, who now doth call. |
| LADY MORE | Ah, my dear husband— |
| MORE | Sweet wife, goodnight, goodnight.<br>God send us all his everlasting light. |
| ROPER | I think before this hour<br>More heavy hearts ne’er parted in the Tower. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [Lady More, Daughters, and Roper one way; More, |
| ROPER | Lieutenant, and Servant as into the Tower]<br><br>Sc. 17 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Sheriffs of [[London]] and their Officers at one door, the Warders with their halberds at another |
| FIRST SHERIFF | Officers, what [[Time\|time]] of day is’t? |
| OFFICER | Almost eight o’[[Clock\|clock]]. |
| SECOND SHERIFF | We must make haste then, lest we stay too long. |
| FIRST WARDER | Good morrow, Master Sheriffs of [[London]]. Master<br>Lieutenant<br>Wills ye repair to the limits of the Tower,<br>There to receive your prisoner. |
| FIRST SHERIFF | (to Officer)<br>Go back and tell his worship we are ready. |
| SECOND SHERIFF | Go bid the officers make clear the way,<br>There may be passage for the prisoner. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lieutenant and his guard, with More |
| MORE | Yet God be thanked, here’s a fair day toward<br>To take our [[Journey\|journey]] in. Master Lieutenant,<br>It were fair walking on the Tower leads. |
| LIEUTENANT | An so it might have liked my sovereign lord,<br>I would to God you might have walked there still.<br>He weeps |
| MORE | Sir, we are walking to a better place.<br>O sir, your kind and loving [[Tears\|tears]]<br>Are like sweet odours to embalm your friend.<br>Thank your good lady; since I was your guest<br>She has made me a very wanton, in good sooth. |
| LIEUTENANT | O, I had hoped we should not yet have parted! |
| MORE | But I must leave ye for a little while. |
| *(didascalia)* | Within an hour or two you may look for me. |
| MORE | But there will be so many come to see me<br>That I shall be so proud I will not speak;<br>And sure my [[Memory\|memory]] is grown so ill<br>I [[Fear\|fear]] I shall forget my head behind me. |
| LIEUTENANT | God and his blessed angels be about ye!—<br>Here, Master Sheriffs, receive your prisoner. |
| MORE | Good morrow, Master Sheriffs of [[London]], to ye both.<br>I thank ye that ye will vouchsafe to meet me.<br>I see by this you have not quite forgot<br>That I was in times past as you are now,<br>A sheriff of [[London]]. |
| FIRST SHERIFF | Sir, then you know our duty doth require it. |
| MORE | I know it [[Well\|well]], sir, else I would have been glad<br>You might have saved a labour at this [[Time\|time]].<br>[To Second Sheriff] Ah, Master Sheriff,<br>You and I have been of old acquaintance.<br>You were a patient auditor of mine<br>When I read the divinity lecture<br>At St Laurence’s. |
| SECOND SHERIFF | [[Sir Thomas More]],<br>I have heard you oft, as many other did,<br>To our great comfort. |
| MORE | Pray God you may so now, with all my heart.<br>And, as I call to mind,<br>When I studied the law in [[Lincoln]]’s Inn<br>I was of counsel with ye in a cause. |
| SECOND SHERIFF | I was about to say so, good Sir Thomas.<br>〈 〉<br>[They pass over the stage. A ladder to the scaffold is<br><br>revealed, with the Hangman attending it] |
| MORE | O, is this the place?<br>I promise ye, it is a goodly scaffold.<br>In sooth, I am come about a headless errand,<br>For I have not much to say, now I am here.<br>[[Well]], let’s ascend, i’ God’s name.<br>In troth, methinks your stair is somewhat weak.<br>[To the Hangman] I prithee, honest friend, lend me thy<br>hand<br>To help me up. As for my coming down,<br>Let me [[Alone\|alone]], I’ll look to that myself.<br>As he is going up the stairs, enters the Earls of Surrey<br>and Shrewsbury<br>My lords of Surrey and of Shrewsbury, give me your<br>hands yet before we part. Ye see, though it pleaseth the<br>[[King]] to raise me thus high, yet I am not proud; for the<br>higher I mount the better I can see my friends about me.<br>I am now on a far voyage, and this strange wooden<br>[[Horse\|horse]] must bear me thither. Yet I perceive by your looks<br>you like my bargain so ill that there’s not one of ye all<br>dare venture with me. (Walking) Truly, here’s a most<br>sweet gallery. I like the air of it better than my [[Garden\|garden]] at<br>Chelsea. By your patience, good people that have<br>pressed thus into my bedchamber, if you’ll not trouble<br>me I’ll take a sound [[Sleep\|sleep]] here. |
| SHREWSBURY | My lord, ’twere good you’d publish to the world<br>Your great offence unto his majesty. |
| MORE | My lord, I’ll bequeath this legacy to the hangman, and do it instantly.<br>Gives him his gown<br>I confess his majesty hath been ever good to me, and my<br>offence to his highness makes me of a state pleader, a<br>stage player—though I am old and have a bad voice—to<br>act this last scene of my [[Tragedy\|tragedy]]. I’ll send him, for my<br>trespass, a reverend head: somewhat bald, for it is not<br>requisite any head should stand covered to so high<br>majesty. If that content him not, because I think my<br>[[Body\|body]] will then do me small pleasure, let him but bury it<br>and take it. |
| SURREY | My lord, my lord, hold conference with your [[Soul\|soul]].<br>You see, my lord, the [[Time\|time]] of life is short. |
| MORE | I see it, my good lord. I dispatched that business the last [[Night\|night]]. I come hither only to be let [[Blood\|blood]] by the hangman. My doctor here tells me it is good for the headache. |
| HANGMAN | I beseech ye, my lord, forgive me. |
| MORE | Forgive thee, honest fellow? Why? |
| HANGMAN | For your [[Death\|death]], my lord. |
| MORE | O, my [[Death\|death]]! I had rather it were in thy power to forgive me, for thou hast the sharpest action against me. The law, my honest friend, lies in thy hands now. Here’s thy fee.<br>[He gives him] his purse<br>And, my good fellow, let my suit be dispatched presently;<br>for ‘tis all one [[Pain\|pain]] to die a lingering [[Death\|death]] and to live<br>in the continual mill of a lawsuit. But I can tell thee,<br>my neck is so short that if thou shouldst behead an<br>hundred noblemen like myself, thou wouldst ne’er get<br>credit by it. Therefore—look ye, sir—do it handsomely,<br>or, of my word, thou shalt never deal with me<br>hereafter. |
| HANGMAN | I’ll take an order for that, my lord. |
| MORE | One thing more: take heed thou cutt’st not off my beard. O, I forgot, execution passed upon that last [[Night\|night]], and the [[Body\|body]] of it lies buried in the Tower. Stay, is’t not possible to make a scape from all this strong guard? It is.<br>There is a thing within me that will raise<br>And elevate my better part ’bove sight<br>Of these same weaker eyes. And Master Sheriffs,<br>For all this troop of steel that tends my [[Death\|death]],<br>I shall break from you and fly up to heaven.<br>Let’s seek the means for this. |
| HANGMAN | My lord, I pray ye put off your doublet. 115 |
| MORE | Speak not so coldly to me; I am hoarse already.<br>I would be loath, good fellow, to take more.<br>Point me the block; I ne’er was here before. |
| HANGMAN | To the east side, my lord. |
| MORE | Then to the east.<br>We go to sigh; that o’er, to [[Sleep\|sleep]] in rest.<br>Here More forsakes all mirth; good reason why:<br>The [[Fool\|fool]] of flesh must with her frail life die.<br>No eye salute my trunk with a sad tear.<br>Our birth to heaven should be thus: void of [[Fear\|fear]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| SURREY | A very learnèd worthy gentleman<br>Seals error with his [[Blood\|blood]]. Come, we’ll to [[Court\|court]].<br>Let’s sadly hence to perfect unknown fates,<br>Whilst he tends progress to the state of states. [Exeunt]<br><br>[Original Text (Munday)] |
| APPENDIX | A<br><br>The following passages in the Original Text are marked for deletion and replaced by the Additional Passages. |
| AI. | Replaced by 4.1-76. In the Original Text there are missing [[Leaves\|leaves]] between AI and A2. |
| DOLL | Peace there, I say! Hear Captain [[Lincoln]] speak.<br>Keep [[Silence\|silence]] till we know his mind at large. |
| ALL THE REST | Agreed, agreed. Speak then, brave Captain [[Lincoln]]. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | Come, gallant bloods, you whose free souls do scorn<br>To bear th’enforcèd wrongs of aliens.<br>Add rage to resolution. [[Fire]] the houses<br>Of these audacious strangers. This is St Martin’s,<br>And yonder dwells Meautis, a wealthy Piccard,<br>At the Green Gate;<br>De Barde, Peter van Hollak, Adrian Martin,<br>With many more outlandish fugitives.<br>Shall these enjoy more privilege than we<br>In our own country? Let’s then become their slaves.<br>Since justice keeps not them in greater awe,<br>We’ll be ourselves rough ministers at law. |
| ALL THE REST | [[Fire]] the houses, [[Fire\|fire]] the houses! |
| DOLL | Ay, for we may as [[Well\|well]] make bonfires on May Day as at Midsummer. We’ll alter the day in the calendar, and set it down in flaming letters. |
| SHERWIN | Stay, that would much endanger the whole [[City\|city]],<br>Whereto I would not the least prejudice. |
| DOLL | No, nor I neither: so may mine own [[house]] be burned for company. I’ll tell ye what: we’ll drag the strangers out into Moorfields, and there bumbaste them till they stink again. |
| GEORGE BETTS | Let some of us enter the strangers’ houses,<br>And, if we find them there, then bring them forth. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt some, and Sherwin |
| DOLL | If ye bring them forth before ye find them, I’ll never allow of that. |
| WILLIAMSON | Now, lads, how shall we labour in our safety?<br>I hear the Mayor hath gathered men in arms,<br>And that Sheriff More an hour ago received<br>Some of the Privy Council in at Ludgate.<br>Force now must make our peace, or else we fall.<br>’will soon be known we are the principal. |
| DOLL | And what of that? If thou beest afraid, husband, go [[Home\|home]] again and hide thy head, for, by the Lord, I’ll have a little sport now I am at it. |
| GEORGE BETTS | Let’s stand upon our guard, and if they come<br>Receive them as they were our enemies. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sherwin and the rest |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | How now, have ye found any? |
| SHERWIN | Not one, they’re fled. |
| [[Lincoln\|LINCOLN]] | Then [[Fire\|fire]] the houses, that, the Mayor being busy<br>About the quenching of them, we may scape.<br>Burn down their kennels! Let us straight away,<br>Lest that this prove to us an ill May Day. Exeunt |
| *(didascalia)* | Sc. 4a Enter three or four prentices of trades, famongst them Harry, Robin, and Kit, with a pair of cudgels |
| HARRY | Come, lay down the cudgels.—Ha, Robin, you met us [[Well\|well]] at Bunhill, to have you with us a-Maying this morning! |
| ROBIN | [[Faith]], Harry, the head drawer at the Mitre by the Great Conduit called me up, and we went to breakfast into St Anne’s Lane. But come, who begins? In good [[Faith\|faith]], I am clean out of practice. When wast at Garrett’s school, Harry? |
| HARRY | Not this great while; never since I brake his usher’s head when he played his scholar’s prize at the Star in Bread Street. I use all to George Philpot’s at Dowgate. He’s the best back-[[Sword\|sword]] man in England. |
| KIT | ‘Bate me an ace of that,’ quoth Bolton. 58 |
| HARRY | I’ll not bate ye a pin on‘t, sir, for, by this cudgel, ’tis true. |
| KIT | I will cudgel that opinion out of ye. Did you break an usher’s head, sir? |
| HARRY | Ay, marry, did I, sir. |
| KIT | I am very glad on’t. You shall break mine too, an ye can. |
| HARRY | Sirrah, I prithee what art thou? |
| KIT | Why, I am a prentice, as thou art. Seest thou now? I’ll play with thee at blunt here in Cheapside; and when thou hast done, if thou beest angry, I’ll fight with thee at sharp in Moorfields. I have a [[Sword\|sword]] to serve my turn in a favour. ( > come July, to serve < )<br>[Exeunt]<br><br>A2. Replaced approximately by 6.159-63. |
| MORE | To persist in it is present [[Death\|death]]. But if you yield yourselves, no [[Doubt\|doubt]] what punishment you in simplicity have incurred, his highness in [[Mercy\|mercy]] will most graciously pardon.<br><br>A3. Continues after Sc. 7. Replaced by 8.22-46 and 128-159. In the Original Text there are missing [[Leaves\|leaves]] between A3 and A4. Revised Sc. 8 restructures and expands upon A3 and A4. Annotated probably by Hand B.<br><br>This must be new written.<br>Sc. 8 A table being covered with a green carpet, a state cushion on it, and the purse and mace lying thereon, enter [[Sir Thomas More]], and his man Randall with him, attired like him |
| MORE | Come on, sir, are you ready? |
| RANDALL | Yes, my lord. I stand but upon a few points. I shall have done presently. Is it your [[Honour\|honour]]’s pleasure that I should grow proud now? |
| MORE | Ay, I must have thee proud, or else thou‘lt ne’er<br>Be near allied to [[Greatness\|greatness]]. Observe me, sir.<br>The learnèd clerk Erasmus is arrived |
| *(didascalia)* | Within our English [[Court\|court]]. This day, I hear, |
| MORE | He feasteth with an English honoured poet<br>The [[Earl of Surrey]], and I know this [[Night\|night]] 10<br>The famous clerk of Rotterdam will visit<br>[[Sir Thomas More]]. Therefore, sir, act my part.<br>There, take my place furnished with purse and mace.<br>I’ll see if great Erasmus can distinguish<br>Merit and outward ceremony. Observe me, sirrah.<br>I’ll be thy glass. Dress thy behaviour<br>According to my carriage. But beware<br>Thou talk not overmuch, for ‘twill betray thee.<br>Who prates not oft seems wise, his wit few scan,<br>Whilst the tongue blabs tales of th’imperfect man. |
| RANDALL | I conceive your lordship, and have learned your shift so [[Well\|well]] that I must needs be apprehensive.<br>The waits plays within |
| MORE | This music tells us that the Earl is come<br>With learned Erasmus. Now, my Lord Chancellor,<br>Act like a formal player our [[Grave\|grave]] part. |
| RANDALL | I pray ye, my lord, let me command ye to leave me. If I do it not in cue, let your lordship banish me from the wearing of a gold chain for ever. |
| MORE | They come now. Set thy countenance, act thy part<br>With a firm boldness, and thou winnest my heart. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Music. Enter Surrey, Erasmus, and attendants |
| SURREY | Now, great Erasmus, you approach the presence<br>Of a most learned worthy gentleman.<br>This little isle holds not a truer friend<br>Unto the arts; nor doth his [[Greatness\|greatness]] add<br>A feigned flourish to his worthy merit.<br>He’s great in study: that’s the statist’s grace<br>That gains more reverence than the outward place. |
| ERASMUS | Report, my lord, hath crossed the narrow seas,<br>And to the several parts of Christendom<br>Hath borne the fame of your Lord Chancellor.<br>I longed to see him whom with loving thoughts<br>I in my study oft have visited.<br>Is yon Sir Thomas? |
| SURREY | It is, Erasmus.<br>Now do you view the honourablest scholar,<br>The most religious politician,<br>The worthiest counsellor, that tends our state.<br>That study is the general watch of England.<br>In it, the Prince’s safety and the peace<br>That shines upon our commonwealth are forged<br>Upon the golden anvil of his brain.<br>Who cures the realm, such care attends the great<br>That mind and [[Body\|body]] must together sweat. |
| ERASMUS | His lordship hath some weighty business, sure,<br>For see, as yet he takes no notice of us.<br>I think ’were best I do my duty to him<br>In a short Latin speech. |
| SURREY | It will do [[Well\|well]].<br>He’s the best linguist that we have in England. |
| ERASMUS | Cum tua virtus, amplissime doctissimeque vir,<br><br>< > as pervenit < > tu < >visui < > divin<br><br>A4. Replaced by 8.47-127 and 8.219-86. |
| MORE | Methinks this strange and ruffian-like disguise<br>Fits not the follower of a secretary. |
| FALKNER | My lord, I wear my hair upon a vow. |
| SHREWSBURY | But for no penance of your sins, I [[Fear\|fear]]. |
| SURREY | No, he’s no haircloth man, though he wear hair. |
| MORE | Falkner, how long is’t since you cut your locks? |
| FALKNER | Three years, my lord. |
| MORE | How long will’t be before your vow expire? |
| FALKNER | As many years as since my hair was cut. |
| MORE | Sure, vows are holy things, if they be made<br>To good intent; and, sir, you shall not say<br>You were compelled by me to break your vow.<br>But till the expiration of the same,<br>Because I will not have ye walk the streets<br>For every man to stand and [[Wonder\|wonder]] at,<br>I will commit ye prisoner unto Newgate,<br>Except meantime your [[Conscience\|conscience]] give you leave<br>To dispense with the long vow that you have made.—<br>Away with him. |
| SURREY | A cell most meet for such a votary. |
| FALKNER | [[Well]], sir, and I may perhaps be bailed ere’t be long, and yet wear my hair. They lead him out |
| MORE | And Master Sheriff of [[London]],<br>Here in his highness’ name we give you charge<br>Continual watch be kept throughout the [[City\|city]]<br>For the suppressing of these mutinies.<br>And if hereafter any that belong<br>Either to my lord of Winchester or Ely<br>Do come into your [[City\|city]] with a weapon,<br>Or above two of either faction<br>Shall be seen walking in the streets together,<br>Or meet in tavern or in ordinary,<br>They be committed presently to [[Prison\|prison]]. |
| SURREY | And cause to be proclaimed about the [[City\|city]]<br>That no man whatsoever that belongs<br>Either to my lord of Winchester or Ely<br>Do walk without the livery of his lord,<br>Either in cloak or any other garment,<br>That notice may be taken of th’offenders. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Master Morris, and exeunt Sheriff and ail but |
| *(didascalia)* | Morel |
| MORRIS | God save your [[Honour\|honour]] my Lord Chancellor! |
| MORE | Welcome, Master Morris! What news, sir? |
| MORRIS | I come most humbly to entreat your [[Honour\|honour]]<br>In the behalf of a poor man of mine. |
| MORE | What, the votary that will not cut his hair<br>Until the expiration of his vow? |
| MORRIS | My lord, being sorry for his rude behaviour,<br>He hath cut his hair, and doth conform himself<br>To honest decency in his attire. |
| MORE | Where is the fellow? I am glad to hear it.<br>Falkner is brought |
| MORRIS | Here, my good lord. |
| MORE | You mock me, surely. This is not the man. |
| FALKNER | Yes indeed, my lord, I am he. |
| MORE | Thou art not, sure.<br>The other was an ugly, filthy knave;<br>Thou a good-featured and [[Well\|well]]-favoured man.<br>Why, see what monsters you will make yourselves<br>By cherishing a loathsome excrement<br>T’abuse the goodly image of a man<br>Whom God did frame so excellent a creature.<br>[[Well]], be a peaceable and civil man.<br>I do discharge thee. |
| FALKNER | I humbly thank your [[Honour\|honour]]. |
| MORRIS | And myself<br>Shall rest most thankful for this gracious favour. |
| MORE | Will’t please your honours now to keep your way?<br>I [[Fear\|fear]] the lords are hindered by our stay. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Lords |
| MORRIS | See, sir, what your ruffian tricks come to.<br>You think the eye of wisdom does not see<br>Into the brainsick follies of vain heads,<br>But with your swaggering you can bear’t away. |
| FALKNER | Sir, I confess I have been much misgoverned,<br>And led by idle spleens, which now I see<br>Are, like themselves, mere sottish [[Vanity\|vanity]].<br>When in the jail, I better called to mind<br>The [[Grave\|grave]] rebukes of my Lord Chancellor,<br>And looked into myself with more respect<br>Than my rash heat before would let me see.<br>I caused a barber presently be sent for,<br>And moved your worship then to speak for me.<br>But when I fall into like folly again<br>Cashier me < ) |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |
| FALKNER | A5. Replaced by 13.53-122. |
| MORE | Close them not then with tears, for that ostent<br>Gives a wet signal of your discontent.<br>If you will share my fortunes, comfort then:<br>An hundred smiles for one sigh. What, we are men!<br>Resign wet passion to these weaker eyes,<br>Which proves their sex, but grants them ne‘er more<br>wise.<br>Let’s now survey our state. Here sits my wife<br>And dear-esteemed issue. Yonder stand<br>My loving servants. Now the difference<br>Twixt those and these. Now you shall hear me speak<br>Like More in [[Melancholy\|melancholy]]. I conceive that [[Nature\|nature]]<br>Hath sundry metals, out of which she frames<br>Us mortals, each in valuation<br>Outprizing other. Of the finest stuff<br>The finest features come. The rest of earth<br>Receive base fortune even before their birth.<br>Hence slaves have their creation. And I think<br>[[Nature]] provides content for the base mind—<br>Under the whip, the burden, and the toil<br>Their low-wrought bodies drudge in patience—<br>As for the prince, in all his sweet-gorged maw,<br>And his rank flesh that sinfully renews<br>The [[Noon\|noon]]’s excess in the [[Night\|night]]’s dangerous surfeits.<br>What means or misery from our birth doth flow<br>[[Nature]] entitles to us; that we owe.<br>But we, being subject to the rack of hate,<br>Falling from happy life to bondage state,<br>Having seen better days, now know the lack<br>Of glory, that once reared each high-fed back.<br>But you that in your age did ne’er view better,<br>Challenge not fortune for your thriftless debtor. |
| CATESBY | Sir, we have seen far better days than these. |
| MORE | I was the patron of those days, and know<br>Those were but painted days, only for show.<br>Then grieve not you to fall with him that gave them.<br>Pro eris generosis servis gloriosum mori.<br><br>Dear Gough, thou art my learnèd secretary—<br>You, Master Catesby, steward of my [[house]];<br>The rest, like you, have had fair [[Time\|time]] to grow<br>In sunshine of my fortunes. But I must tell ye,<br>Corruption is fled hence with each man’s office—<br>Bribes, that make open traffic twixt the [[Soul\|soul]]<br>And netherland of hell, deliver up<br>Their guilty homage to their second lords.<br>Then, living thus untainted, you are [[Well\|well]].<br>[[Truth]] is no pilot for the land of hell. |
| APPENDIX | B<br><br>The following authorial first drafts are marked for deletion and immediately replaced.<br><br>B1. First Version of 9.334-53 in Add. VI. |
| MORE | [[Lord Mayor]] and ladies and the rest, be patient.<br>The state hath sent, and I must needs be gone.<br>Lead on there.—What seek’st thou, fellow? |
| PLAYER | of WIT Your lordship sent us eight angels by your man, and I have lost one here amongst the rushes. |
| MORE | Eight angels? Who delivered it? I sent them ten. |
| SERVINGMAN | I, my lord, delivered it. Anon they shall have two more. |
| PLAYER | of WIT That’s more than we heard before, my lord. |
| MORE | Am I a man of equity<br>Equally to divide true right his own,<br>And shall I have deceivers in my [[house]]?<br>Go pull the coat over the varlet’s ears.<br>There are too many such.<br>Give them their due. Lead on away.<br><br>B2. First Version of 17.106-127 (Stay... states) in the Original Text.<br>Come, let’s to the block. |
| HANGMAN | My lord, I pray ye put off your doublet. |
| MORE | No, my good friend, I have a great cold already, and I would be loath to take more. Point me meet the block, for I was ne’er here before. |
| HANGMAN | To the east side, my lord. |
| MORE | Then to the east.<br>We go to sigh; that o’er, to [[Sleep\|sleep]] in rest.<br>No eye salute my trunk with a sad tear.<br>Our birth to heaven should be thus: void of [[Fear\|fear]]. Exit |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="scenes--scene_1" data-title="Sir Thomas More — Scene 1" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="scene" data-tags="archetype/king,motif/blood,motif/city,motif/home,motif/time,motif/well,concept/beauty,concept/courage,concept/doubt,concept/faith,concept/poverty,concept/reform,concept/revenge,setting/london,character/lincoln,character/lord_mayor,character/sir_thomas_more"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter at one end John [[Lincoln]] with George Betts and Clown Betts together. At the other end enters Francis de Barde and Doll, a lusty woman, he hauling her by the arm |
| DOLL | Whither wilt thou haul me? |
| BARDE | Whither I please. Thou art my prize, and I plead purchase of thee. |
| DOLL | Purchase of me? Away, ye rascal! I am an honest, plain carpenter’s wife, and, though I have no [[Beauty\|beauty]] to like a husband, yet whatsoever is mine scorns to stoop to a stranger. Hand off then when I bid theel |
| BARDE | Go with me quietly, or I’ll compel thee. |
| DOLL | Compel me, ye dog’s face? Thou think‘st thou hast the goldsmith’s wife in hand, whom thou enticed’st from her husband with all his plate, and when thou turned‘st her [[Home\|home]] to him again mad’st him, like an ass, pay for his wife’s board. |
| BARDE | So will I make thy husband too, if please me. |
